*   Visited: `{L}`

**Level 0 (Length 0):**
*   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.

*   Queue: `[(M, [L, M]), (E, [L, E])]`

*   Enqueue `(B, [L, E, B])`.
*   Visited: `{L, M, E, F, B}`.
*   Queue: `[(F, [L, M, F]), (B, [L, E, B])]`

**Level 2 (Length 2):**
*   Dequeue `(F, [L, M, F])`.

    *   Is F directly connected to X? Yes.
    *   Is F directly connected to G? Yes.
    *   Is F directly connected to K? Yes.
*   Enqueue `(X, [L, M, F, X])`, `(G, [L, M, F, G])`, `(K, [L, M, F, K])`.
*   Visited: `{L, M, E, F, B, X, G, K}`.
*   Queue: `[(B, [L, E, B]), (X, [L, M, F, X]), (G, [L, M, F, G]), (K, [L, M, F, K])]`

*   Dequeue `(B, [L, E, B])`.

    *   Is B directly connected to N? Yes.

*   Enqueue `(H, [L, E, B, H])`, `(N, [L, E, B, N])`, `(C, [L, E, B, C])`.

*   Queue: `[(X, [L, M, F, X]), (G, [L, M, F, G]), (K, [L, M, F, K]), (H, [L, E, B, H]), (N, [L, E, B, N]), (C, [L, E, B, C])]`

*   Dequeue `(G, [L, M, F, G])`.
*   Neighbors of G: F (visited), X (visited), H (visited), K (visited). No new unvisited nodes.

*   Dequeue `(K, [L, M, F, K])`.
*   Neighbors of K: J (TARGET!), F (visited), G (visited), X (visited).
    *   Is K directly connected to J? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
*   **Target J found!** The path is `[L, M, F, K, J]`.
*   The length of this path is 4 (L-M, M-F, F-K, K-J).

Since this is a breadth-first search, the first path found to the target node is guaranteed to be the shortest path.

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are L and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between L and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node L (black) is connected to E and M.
    *   Node J (black) is connected to M, I, P, and D.

2.  **Consider paths originating from L:**
    Any path from L to J must start by traversing either the edge (L, E) or the edge (L, M).

3.  **Evaluate a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove only the edge (L, E): Node L is still connected to M. A path like L-M-J still exists. Therefore, removing (L, E) alone is not sufficient.
    *   If I remove only the edge (L, M): Node L is still connected to E. A path like L-E-I-J still exists. Therefore, removing (L, M) alone is not sufficient.
    *   Since removing any single edge does not disconnect L from J, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Evaluate a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider removing both edges connected to L: (L, E) and (L, M).
        *   If (L, E) is removed, L can no longer reach E.
        *   If (L, M) is removed, L can no longer reach M.
        *   After removing both (L, E) and (L, M), node L becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
        *   If L is isolated, no path can exist from L to J.
    *   This cut set `[(L, E), (L, M)]` successfully disconnects L from J and has a size of 2.

5.  **Confirm minimality:**
    Since we established that 1 edge is not sufficient, and we found a cut set of 2 edges that achieves disconnection, 2 is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, E), (L, M)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['E', 'L'], ['L', 'M']]}

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and D. Our goal is to disconnect E from D.
2.  **Analyze Node D's Connections:** I start by examining node D, as it has a relatively small number of connections. Node D is directly connected to two other nodes: J and P. This means there are only two edges directly incident to D: (J, D) and (P, D).
3.  **Identify All Paths to D:** Any path from node E to node D must, by definition, use either the edge (J, D) or the edge (P, D) as its final segment to reach D.
4.  **Consider Removing Edges Incident to D:**
    *   If I remove only the edge (J, D), then paths from E to D that go through P (e.g., E-B-C-I-P-D) would still exist.
    *   If I remove only the edge (P, D), then paths from E to D that go through J (e.g., E-M-J-D) would still exist.
    *   Therefore, to completely disconnect D from the rest of the graph (and thus from E), both edges (J, D) and (P, D) must be removed.
5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** Removing both (J, D) and (P, D) results in D being completely isolated. This means no path can exist between E and D. Since removing only one edge is insufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Image name: PGS_task2_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect M and B. I cannot remove M or B.

First, I will identify several node-disjoint paths between M and B. If I can find `k` node-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum cut) must be at least `k`.

Let's trace paths from M to B:

1.  **Path 1: M - E - B**
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding M and B): {E}

2.  **Path 2: M - L - D - J - I - C - B**
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding M and B): {L, D, J, I, C}

3.  **Path 3: M - F - G - H - B**
    *   Nodes in this path (excluding M and B): {F, G, H}

Let's check if these three paths are node-disjoint (apart from M and B):
*   Nodes from Path 1: {E}
*   Nodes from Path 2: {L, D, J, I, C}
*      Nodes from Path 3: {F, G, H}

Comparing these sets, they are indeed disjoint. No node (other than M or B) is shared between any two of these three paths.
Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths between M and B, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3 and verify that it disconnects M and B.
Based on the identified paths, a potential cut set could be {E, J, H}.
Let's verify if removing these three nodes disconnects M and B:

1.  **Paths starting M -> E:**
    *   The path M - E - B is directly broken by the removal of node E.

2.  **Paths starting M -> L:**
    *   All paths from M through L must pass through D and then J. For example:
        *   M - L - D - J - I - C - B
        *   M - L - D - J - P - I - C - B
        *   M - L - D - J - P - O - C - B
        *   M - L - D - J - P - O - N - B
        *   M - L - D - J - P - O - Y - N - B
    *   Since node J is removed, all these paths are broken.

3.  **Paths starting M -> F:**
    *   From F, paths can go to G or X.
        *   **Through G:**
            *   M - F - G - H - B: This path is broken by the removal of node H.
            *   M - F - G - K - J - ... : Any path continuing from K must go through J (K is only connected to J). Since J is removed, these paths are broken.
        *   **Through X:**
            *   M - F - X - G - H - B: This path is broken by the removal of node H.
            *   M - F - X - G - K - J - ... : Any path continuing from K must go through J. Since J is removed, these paths are broken.

All identified paths from M to B are broken by the removal of nodes {E, J, H}.
Since we established that at least 3 nodes must be removed, and we found a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects M and B, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [E, J, H]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'E']}

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, E and J.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are E and J.

2.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J:**
    To determine the minimum cut, it's helpful to consider the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and J (Menger's Theorem). If we can find N edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least N.

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from E to J:
    *   **Path 1 (P1): E - M - J**
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): E - H - I - J**
        Edges: {(E, H), (H, I), (I, J)}

    *   **Path 3 (P3): E - G - H - B - C - O - P - J**
        Edges: {(E, G), (G, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in P1: (E, M), (M, J)
    *   Edges in P2: (E, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   Edges in P3: (E, G), (G, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Comparing the sets of edges, there are no common edges between P1, P2, and P3. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.
    According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, then at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes. This means the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges and verify its effectiveness:**
    Now that we know the minimum is at least 3, we need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and J.

    Consider the following cut set: **[(M, J), (H, I), (H, B)]**

    Let's verify if removing these three edges breaks all paths from E to J:
    *   **Paths through M to J:**
        *   E - M - J: Broken by removing (M, J).
        *   E - L - M - J: Broken by removing (M, J).
        (All paths from E to J that pass through M are cut by (M, J)).

    *   **Paths through H to I to J (or via P):**
        *   E - H - I - J: Broken by removing (H, I).
        *   E - G - H - I - J: Broken by removing (H, I).
        *   E - F - G - H - I - J: Broken by removing (H, I).
        *   E - H - I - P - J: Broken by removing (H, I).
        *   E - G - H - I - P - J: Broken by removing (H, I).
        *   E - F - G - H - I - P - J: Broken by removing (H, I).
        (All paths from E to J that pass through H and then I are cut by (H, I)).

    *   **Paths through H to B to J (or via C, N, O, P, D):**
        *   E - H - B - C - I - J: Broken by removing (H, B).
        *   E - H - B - C - O - P - J: Broken by removing (H, B).
        *   E - H - B - N - C - I - J: Broken by removing (H, B).
        *   E - H - B - N - O - P - J: Broken by removing (H, B).
        *   E - H - B - N - Y - O - P - J: Broken by removing (H, B).
        (Similarly for paths starting E-G-H-B...J and E-F-G-H-B...J, all are cut by (H, B)).

    *   **Paths through D to J:**
        Any path from E to J that uses the edge (D, J) must first reach D. The only way to reach D from E is through P (since D is only connected to J and P).
        So, we need to check if E can reach P.
        *   Paths to P from E: E - H - I - P (broken by (H, I)).
        *   Paths to P from E: E - H - B - C - O - P (broken by (H, B)).
        *   Paths to P from E: E - H - B - N - O - P (broken by (H, B)).
        *   Paths to P from E: E - H - B - N - Y - O - P (broken by (H, B)).
        Since all paths from E to P are broken by either (H, I) or (H, B), E cannot reach P. Therefore, E cannot reach D, and thus cannot reach J via D.

    All identified paths from E to J are broken by the removal of edges (M, J), (H, I), and (H, B).

4.  **Conclusion:**
    We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, which implies a minimum cut of at least 3.
    We have also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects E and J.
    Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The final answer is in the specified format.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (H, I), (H, B)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['G', 'H']], [['E', 'B'], ['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E']]]}

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are H and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H from N. I cannot remove H or N.

First, I will try to find a cut set of size 1.
*   If I remove B: Paths like H-I-C-N or H-E-M-J-P-O-N still exist. So B is not enough.
*   If I remove C: Paths like H-B-N or H-I-P-O-N still exist. So C is not enough.
*   If I remove O: Paths like H-B-N or H-I-C-N still exist. So O is not enough.
*   If I remove Y: Paths like H-B-N or H-I-C-N still exist. So Y is not enough.
*   If I remove E: Paths like H-B-N or H-I-C-N still exist. So E is not enough.
*   If I remove G: Paths like H-B-N or H-I-C-N still exist. So G is not enough.
*   If I remove I: Paths like H-B-N or H-E-M-J-P-O-N still exist. So I is not enough.
Since no single node can disconnect H and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

Next, I will try to find a cut set of size 2.
Let's consider the main "entry points" from H to the rest of the graph: B, E, G, I.
Let's consider the main "exit points" from the rest of the graph to N: B, C, O, Y.
Note that paths through G must pass through F and then E to reach N's side (e.g., H-G-F-E-...). So G is not a direct bottleneck. Y is only connected to N, so it's not an intermediate node for paths from H.

Effectively, all paths from H to N must pass through one of {B, E, I} and one of {B, C, O}.

Let's try removing pairs of nodes:
*   If I remove [B, C]: Paths like H-I-P-O-N or H-E-M-J-P-O-N still exist. Not enough.
*   If I remove [B, O]: Paths like H-I-C-N or H-E-M-J-P-C-N still exist. Not enough.
*   If I remove [E, I]: The path H-B-N still exists. Not enough.
*   If I remove [B, E]: Paths like H-I-C-N or H-I-P-O-N still exist. Not enough.
*   If I remove [B, I]: Paths like H-E-M-J-P-O-N or H-E-M-J-P-C-N still exist. Not enough.

Since no pair of nodes seems to disconnect H and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3. This suggests looking for 3 internally vertex-disjoint paths between H and N.

Let's identify three internally vertex-disjoint paths:
1.  **Path 1:** H - B - N
    *   Internal node: B
2.  **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
    *   Internal nodes: I, C
3.  **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
    *   Internal nodes: E, M, J, P, O

Let's verify if these paths are internally vertex-disjoint:
*   The internal node B from Path 1 is not present in Path 2 or Path 3.
*   The internal nodes I and C from Path 2 are not present in Path 1 or Path 3.
*   The internal nodes E, M, J, P, and O from Path 3 are not present in Path 1 or Path 2.
Since these three paths are internally vertex-disjoint, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H and N.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3. Based on the identified paths, a natural candidate for a cut set is to pick one internal node from each path. For example, [B, I, E].

Let's verify if removing nodes [B, I, E] disconnects H and N:
1.  **Remove B:** This breaks Path 1 (H-B-N).
2.  **Remove I:** This breaks Path 2 (H-I-C-N) and any other path that uses I (e.g., H-E-I-C-N, H-I-P-O-N, H-E-M-L-I-C-N).
3.  **Remove E:** This breaks Path 3 (H-E-M-J-P-O-N) and any other path that uses E (e.g., H-E-M-J-P-C-N, H-G-F-E-I-C-N).

After removing B, I, and E:
*   H is now connected to G.
*   G is connected to F, K, X.
*   F is connected to G, K, X. (F was also connected to E, but E is removed).
*   K is connected to G, F, X.
*   X is connected to G, F, K.
This forms a component {H, G, F, K, X}. None of these nodes are connected to N or any of N's remaining neighbors (C, O, Y) without passing through B, I, or E (which are all removed).
Therefore, H is completely disconnected from N.

Since we found 3 internally vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 ([B, I, E]) that successfully disconnects H and N.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, E]

    *   Node I is directly connected to H, B, C, E, J, and P.
4.  **Identify Main Path Bundles:** I observe that all paths from G to I can be categorized into two main "bundles" based on their initial connection from G:
    *   **Bundle 1: Paths through H.** These paths start G -> H -> ... -> I. Examples include G-H-I, G-H-B-I, G-H-B-C-I, G-H-B-C-N-O-P-I, G-H-B-C-O-P-I.

5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Set:**
    *   To disconnect G and I, both bundles of paths must be severed.
    *   To sever **Bundle 1**, node H must be removed, as it is the critical link for all paths in this bundle.
    *   To sever **Bundle 2**, we need to break the connection from F to I. Since all paths from F to I must pass through E, removing either F or E would effectively sever this bundle.

        *   This set also successfully disconnects G and I.

1.  **Path 1:** H - I - P - O
    (Intermediate nodes: I, P)

2.  **Path 2:** H - B - N - O
    (Intermediate nodes: B, N)

    (Intermediate nodes: G, F, E, C)

According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is 3. If I can only find 2 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is 2.

Since I can only find two edge-disjoint paths, Menger's Theorem implies that the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Let's analyze the "entry points" to E:
- Node P is connected to E.
- Node B is connected to E.
- Node M is connected to E.
- Node L is connected to E. However, L is only connected to E and M. This means any path from H to E that uses L must first pass through M (e.g., H -> ... -> M -> L -> E). Therefore, if M is removed, L is effectively disconnected from any path originating from H. So, the critical "entry points" to E are P, B, and M.

Now, let's look for vertex-disjoint paths between H and E. If I can find 'k' vertex-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove will be at least 'k'.

Here are three vertex-disjoint paths (excluding H and E):
1.  **H - G - F - P - E**
    Nodes in this path (excluding H and E): {G, F, P}
2.  **H - I - J - M - E**
    Nodes in this path (excluding H and E): {I, J, M}
3.  **H - C - B - E**
    Nodes in this path (excluding H and E): {C, B}

Let's verify that these paths are vertex-disjoint:
- Path 1 uses {G, F, P}.
- Path 2 uses {I, J, M}.
- Path 3 uses {C, B}.
There are no common nodes among these three sets. Therefore, these three paths are indeed vertex-disjoint.

Since there are at least three vertex-disjoint paths between H and E, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect them must be at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3. Based on the "entry points" to E (P, B, M), let's consider removing these three nodes.

Let's verify if removing nodes P, B, and M disconnects H from E:
1.  **Removing P:** This severs all paths that use P to reach E. This includes paths like H-G-F-P-E, H-I-P-E, H-I-J-P-E, H-I-C-N-O-P-E, H-I-C-O-P-E, H-C-N-O-P-E, H-C-O-P-E, H-C-I-P-E, H-C-I-J-P-E, and any other path that passes through P.
2.  **Removing B:** This severs all paths that use B to reach E. This includes paths like H-I-C-B-E, H-I-C-N-B-E, H-I-C-O-N-B-E, H-C-B-E, H-C-N-B-E, H-C-O-N-B-E, and any other path that passes through B.
3.  **Removing M:** This severs all paths that use M to reach E. This includes paths like H-I-J-M-E, H-C-I-J-M-E. As established earlier, any path using L to reach E must also pass through M (H -> ... -> M -> L -> E), so removing M also effectively disconnects L from H's side.

Since P, B, and M are the only effective "entry points" to E from the rest of the graph, removing these three nodes will ensure that no path exists between H and E.

Therefore, a cut set of size 3, [P, B, M], successfully disconnects H and E. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, B, M]

-   Path 3 uses intermediate nodes: {C, B}

2.  **Analyze Connectivity of Target Node G:** Node G is part of a small sub-graph (a clique) with nodes X, K, and F. The edges within this clique are (G,X), (G,K), (G,F), (X,K), (X,F), (K,F).
3.  **Identify Exit Points from G's Sub-graph:** To reach node N, any path originating from G must first leave this {G, X, K, F} sub-graph. I need to identify all edges that connect this sub-graph to the rest of the graph.
    *   Node G is connected to H via edge (G, H).
    *   Node F is connected to E via edge (F, E).
    *   Nodes X and K have no direct connections outside the {G, X, K, F} sub-graph other than to G and F.
    Therefore, any path from G to N must traverse either the edge (G, H) or the edge (F, E) (after potentially moving within the {G, X, K, F} clique).
4.  **Propose a Cut Set:** If we remove both (G, H) and (F, E), then G (and its entire clique {G, X, K, F}) will be completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, including N. This set of edges is [(G, H), (F, E)].
5.  **Determine Size of Proposed Cut Set:** The proposed cut set contains 2 edges.
6.  **Verify Minimality (Check for Cut of Size 1):**
    *   If only (G, H) is removed: Paths from G to N can still exist via G-F-E-...-N. For example, G-F-E-I-C-N.
    *   If only (F, E) is removed: Paths from G to N can still exist via G-H-...-N. For example, G-H-B-C-N.
    Since removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect G from N, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
7.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we have found a cut of size 2 that successfully disconnects G from N, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

        *   E-I-H (broken by removing I)

    *   All other paths from E to H also rely on B, I, or G to reach H, so they will also be broken.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from B. This is a min-cut problem, and by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and B.

First, I will identify as many edge-disjoint paths as possible between H and B.

1.  **Path 1 (P1): H - B**
    Edges: `{(H, B)}`

2.  **Path 2 (P2): H - E - B**
    Edges: `{(H, E), (E, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from P1.

3.  **Path 3 (P3): H - I - B**
    Edges: `{(H, I), (I, B)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.

4.  **Path 4 (P4): H - G - F - E - I - C - B**
    Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.
    Edges of P4: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, C), (C, B)}`
    *   P1 uses `(H, B)`. No overlap with P4.
    *   P2 uses `(H, E)` and `(E, B)`. No overlap with P4.
    *   P3 uses `(H, I)` and `(I, B)`. No overlap with P4.
    This path is indeed edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that, when removed, completely disconnects H from B. I will try to select edges that cut one edge from each of these 4 edge-disjoint paths.

Consider the following set of 4 edges:
*   `(H, B)` (cuts P1)
*   `(H, E)` (cuts P2)
*   `(H, I)` (cuts P3)
*   `(F, E)` (cuts P4, specifically the segment H-G-F-E)

Let's verify if removing these 4 edges `{(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)}` disconnects H from B:

1.  **Edges removed:** `(H, B)`, `(H, E)`, `(H, I)`, `(F, E)`.

2.  **Analyze H's remaining connections:**
    After removing `(H, B)`, `(H, E)`, `(H, I)`, node H is now only connected to `G` and `F`.

3.  **Check if G or F can reach B:**
    *   **From G:** G is connected to F, X, K.
        *   G-F: F is connected to G, K, X. (F is no longer connected to E because `(F, E)` was removed).
        *   G-X: X is connected to K, F, G.
        *   G-K: K is connected to F, X.
        None of these nodes (G, F, X, K) are B, nor are they connected to B or any path leading to B after the specified edges are removed. Specifically, the path H-G-F-E-I-C-B is broken at (F, E).

    *   **From F:** F is connected to G, K, X. (F is no longer connected to E because `(F, E)` was removed).
        None of these nodes (G, K, X) lead to B.

Since H can only reach G, F, K, and X, and none of these nodes can reach B after the removal of the specified edges, H is completely disconnected from B.

Therefore, a cut set of 4 edges has been found, and since we established that at least 4 edges are required, this is the minimum.

The final answer is in the specified format.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and J. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from J. The target nodes E and J cannot be removed.

2.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Nodes directly connected to E: M, L, F, B.
    *   Nodes directly connected to J: M, I, P.

3.  **Analyze the structure of paths:** Any path from E to J must pass through one of E's neighbors and one of J's neighbors. More importantly, any path from E to J must pass through one of the nodes directly connected to J (M, I, or P).

4.  **Test for a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove M: The path E-M-J is broken. However, paths like E-B-C-I-J and E-B-C-N-O-P-J still exist. So, M alone is not sufficient.
    *   If I remove I: Paths through I to J are broken. However, paths like E-M-J and E-B-C-N-O-P-J still exist. So, I alone is not sufficient.
    *   If I remove P: Paths through P to J are broken. However, paths like E-M-J and E-B-C-I-J still exist. So, P alone is not sufficient.
    *   Since no single node can disconnect E and J, the minimum cut set size is at least 2.

5.  **Test for a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider removing any two of the nodes directly connected to J: {M, I, P}.
        *   If I remove M and I:
            *   The path E-M-J is broken (by M).
            *   All paths ending in -I-J are broken (by I).
            *   However, the path E-B-C-N-O-P-J still exists, as P is not removed. So, [M, I] is not a cut set.
        *   If I remove M and P:
            *   The path E-M-J is broken (by M).
            *   All paths ending in -P-J are broken (by P).
            *   However, the path E-B-C-I-J still exists, as I is not removed. So, [M, P] is not a cut set.
        *   If I remove I and P:
            *   All paths ending in -I-J or -P-J are broken (by I and P).
            *   However, the path E-M-J still exists, as M is not removed. So, [I, P] is not a cut set.
    *   Since removing any two of {M, I, P} is not sufficient, and these are the only nodes directly connected to J, it implies that all three (M, I, P) are critical for different sets of paths. Therefore, the minimum cut set size must be at least 3.

6.  **Test for a cut set of size 3:**
    *   Consider removing M, I, and P.
        *   Any path from E to J must pass through M, I, or P, because these are the only nodes directly connected to J.
        *   If M is removed, all paths E-...-M-J are broken.
        *   If I is removed, all paths E-...-I-J (and E-...-I-P-J) are broken.
        *   If P is removed, all paths E-...-P-J (and E-...-I-P-J) are broken.
        *   Therefore, removing M, I, and P will completely disconnect E from J.

7.  **Conclusion:** A cut set of size 3 ([M, I, P]) has been found, and it has been demonstrated that no cut set of size 1 or 2 exists. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H and E. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect H and E.

1.  **Identify all direct connections for the target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.
    *   Node E is connected to: M, F, B, L.

2.  **Consider the degree of the target nodes:**
    *   Degree of H is 3 (edges: (H, G), (H, I), (H, B)).
    *   Degree of E is 4 (edges: (E, M), (E, F), (E, B), (E, L)).

3.  **Initial estimation of cut size:**
    *   If we remove all edges connected to H, H becomes isolated. This would require removing 3 edges: {(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)}. This is a valid cut set.
    *   If we remove all edges connected to E, E becomes isolated. This would require removing 4 edges: {(E, M), (E, F), (E, B), (E, L)}.
    *   Since we found a cut of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    To confirm if 3 is indeed the minimum, we can try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and E. If we find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least *k*.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from H to E:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - E
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, E)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - E
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E)}
        (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as none of its edges are (H,B) or (B,E)).

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - N - O - Y - L - E
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N), (N, O), (O, Y), (Y, L), (L, E)}
        (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, as none of its edges are shared with the previous two paths).

5.  **Conclusion from edge-disjoint paths:**
    We have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E. According to Menger's Theorem (edge version), the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and E is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

6.  **Confirm the minimal cut set:**
    Since we know the minimum is at least 3, and we previously identified a cut set of size 3 (by removing all edges connected to H), this set must be a minimal cut.
    The cut set is: {(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)}.
    Removing these three edges ensures that H has no outgoing connections, thus completely disconnecting it from E and the rest of the graph.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]

First, I identify the neighbors of M and B:
*   Neighbors of M: L, E, J
*   Neighbors of B: N, C, H, E

    *   From P, I can go to O.

    *   I verify that P3 does not share any edges with P1 or P2. All three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

    *   If I remove nodes B and E: Paths like H-I-C-O still exist.

    *   If I remove nodes I and C: Paths like H-B-N-O and H-F-E-M-J-P-O still exist.
    By trying various combinations, it appears that no two nodes are sufficient to disconnect H and O. This suggests the minimum cut set might be 3 or more.

4.  **Find Vertex-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm that a cut set of size 2 is not possible, I will try to find 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - O (Intermediate nodes: B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - O (Intermediate nodes: I, C)
    *   **Path 3:** H - F - E - M - J - P - O (Intermediate nodes: F, E, M, J, P)

    Let's verify that the intermediate nodes of these three paths are mutually disjoint:
    *   Intermediate nodes for Path 1: {B, N}
    *   Intermediate nodes for Path 2: {I, C}
    *   Intermediate nodes for Path 3: {F, E, M, J, P}
    These three sets of nodes are indeed disjoint. Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and O, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect them is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Now that I know the minimum is at least 3, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H from O.
    Consider removing nodes **B, I, and E**:

    Therefore, removing nodes B, I, and E will completely disconnect H from O. This is a cut set of size 3.

The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, G and I. This is a minimum s-t cut problem, where s=G and t=I. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges in an s-t cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint s-t paths.

First, I will identify all edges in the graph:
Edges connected to G: (G, X), (G, H), (G, F), (G, K)
Edges connected to I: (I, H), (I, J), (I, C), (I, P)
Other edges:
(X, K), (X, F)
(K, M)
(F, E)
(E, M), (E, L), (E, B)
(H, B)
(B, C), (B, N)
(C, N), (C, O)
(N, O)
(M, J)
(J, P)
(P, D), (P, O)
(O, Y)

Next, I will search for edge-disjoint paths from G to I. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edge.

1.  **Path 1 (P1): G - H - I**
    Edges: (G, H), (H, I)

2.  **Path 2 (P2): G - K - M - J - I**
    Edges: (G, K), (K, M), (M, J), (J, I)
    This path does not share any edges with P1.

3.  **Path 3 (P3): G - F - E - B - C - I**
    Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I)
    This path does not share any edges with P1 or P2.

Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths from G to I, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G and I.
Consider the following set of edges:
*   **(G, H)**: This edge cuts Path 1 (G - H - I).
*   **(K, M)**: This edge cuts Path 2 (G - K - M - J - I).
*   **(F, E)**: This edge cuts Path 3 (G - F - E - B - C - I).

Let's verify if removing these three edges completely disconnects G from I:
1.  **Remove (G, H)**: Any path starting G-H-... is broken.
2.  **Remove (K, M)**: Any path starting G-K-M-... is broken. Also, any path starting G-X-K-M-... is broken.
3.  **Remove (F, E)**: Any path starting G-F-E-... is broken. Also, any path starting G-X-F-E-... is broken.

Let's consider all possible ways to leave the immediate neighborhood of G:
*   Through H: (G, H) is removed. No path.
*   Through K: (G, K) is still present. From K, one can go to X or M.
    *   If K goes to M, (K, M) is removed. No path.
    *   If K goes to X, (K, X) is still present. From X, one can go to F or G.
        *   If X goes to F, (X, F) is still present. From F, one can go to E or G.
            *   If F goes to E, (F, E) is removed. No path.
            *   If F goes to G, this is a cycle, not a path to I.
        *   If X goes to G, this is a cycle.
*   Through F: (G, F) is still present. From F, one can go to X or E.
    *   If F goes to E, (F, E) is removed. No path.
    *   If F goes to X, (F, X) is still present. From X, one can go to K or G.
        *   If X goes to K, (X, K) is still present. From K, one can go to M or G.
            *   If K goes to M, (K, M) is removed. No path.
            *   If K goes to G, this is a cycle.
        *   If X goes to G, this is a cycle.
*   Through X: (G, X) is still present. From X, one can go to K or F. (As analyzed above, both lead to dead ends due to removed edges).

This confirms that the set of edges {(G, H), (K, M), (F, E)} successfully disconnects G from I. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and this cut set has 3 edges, it is the minimum possible.

    *   Node B is directly connected to C, N, O, and E.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate H, I can remove all edges connected to it: (H,I), (H,G), and (H,X). This would be a cut set of size 3.
    *   To isolate B, I can remove all edges connected to it: (B,C), (B,N), (B,O), and (B,E). This would be a cut set of size 4.
    *   Since 3 is less than 4, a cut set of size 3 is a potential minimum. I need to check if a cut set of size 2 is possible.

3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes (s-t cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least 3, meaning a cut of size 2 is not possible.

    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from H to B:

    *   **Path 1:** H-I-C-B
        Edges used: `{(H,I), (I,C), (C,B)}`

    *   **Path 2:** H-G-F-E-B

        Let's check if Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1. The sets of edges `{(H,I), (I,C), (C,B)}` and `{(H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,B)}` have no common edges. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, I need to find a third path from H to B that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used by Path 1 and Path 2 combined: `{(H,I), (I,C), (C,B), (H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,B)}`.

        From K, I can go to F or G. (F and G are used in Path 2).
        Let's try H-X-K-F.
        From F, I can go to E, C, G, L. (E, C, G are used in Path 1 or 2).
        Let's try H-X-K-F-L.
        From L, I can go to E or M. (E is used in Path 2).
        Let's try H-X-K-F-L-M.
        From M, I can go to J, O, L. (L is used).
        Let's try H-X-K-F-L-M-O.
        From O, I can go to Y, N, B, M, P. (M is used).
        Let's try H-X-K-F-L-M-O-B.
        Edges used by Path 3: `{(H,X), (X,K), (K,F), (F,L), (L,M), (M,O), (O,B)}`.

        Let's verify if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        *   Path 3 vs Path 1: `{(H,X), (X,K), (K,F), (F,L), (L,M), (M,O), (O,B)}` vs `{(H,I), (I,C), (C,B)}`. No common edges.
        *   Path 3 vs Path 2: `{(H,X), (X,K), (K,F), (F,L), (L,M), (M,O), (O,B)}` vs `{(H,G), (G,F), (F,E), (E,B)}`. No common edges.

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths (Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3), by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

4.  **Confirm the minimum cut:**

This is a cut set of size 3. Since we've proven that the minimum cut must be at least 3, this cut set is indeed minimal.

    *   The paths E - M - J and E - L - M - J are blocked by removing M.
    *   All other paths (starting E-H, E-G, E-F) eventually converge on H to proceed towards J. For example, E-G-H-I-J, E-F-H-I-J, etc. These paths are blocked by removing H.

    *   **Can a single node disconnect E and J?**
        *   If I remove M, paths like E - H - I - J still exist.
        *   If I remove H, paths like E - M - J still exist.

        Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

    *   **Consider removing M and H:**
        *   Removing M breaks all paths that go through M (E-M-J, E-L-M-J).
        *   Removing H breaks all paths that go through H (E-H-I-J, E-H-I-P-J, E-H-B-C-I-J, E-H-B-C-O-P-J, E-H-B-N-O-P-J, E-H-B-N-Y-O-P-J, and all paths starting E-G or E-F that lead to H).
        Since every path from E to J must pass through either M or H, removing both M and H will completely disconnect E from J.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the cut set is [M, H].

    *   Intermediate nodes for Path 3: {F, E, B, C}
    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive. Therefore, I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect G and I must be at least 3.

3.  **Propose a cut set of size 3:** Based on the three vertex-disjoint paths, I need to select one node from each path's set of intermediate nodes to form a cut set. A possible cut set is [H, P, B]:
    *   H from Path 1 (G - H - I)
    *   P from Path 2 (G - X - K - J - P - I)
    *   B from Path 3 (G - F - E - B - C - I)

    *   **Remaining neighbors of I:** C (since H and P are removed).

        *   F - X: (Already covered above, leads to K-J, which does not connect to C).

    Since all paths from G (via X or F) to I (via C) are broken by the removal of H, P, and B, this cut set successfully disconnects G and I.

2.  **Analyze the connections of G's neighbors to the rest of the graph (excluding G):**

    *   **K:** Connected to G, F, X. All connections of K (other than to G) are to F and X, which are also neighbors of G.
    *   **F:** Connected to G, K, X, P, E. Nodes P and E are part of the "N-side" of the graph.
    *   **X:** Connected to G, K, F. All connections of X (other than to G) are to K and F, which are also neighbors of G.

3.  **Determine the "entry points" to G's immediate neighborhood from the N-side:**
    Based on the analysis in step 2, any path from N to G must eventually reach either H or F, as K and X only connect to G, F, and each other. This means any path from N to K or X must first pass through F.
    *   Paths to G via H: These paths use the edge (H, G).
    *   Paths to G via F: These paths use the edge (F, G).
    *   Paths to G via K: These paths must come from F (e.g., N-...-F-K-G). If (F,G) is cut, then F is disconnected from G. Since K's only connection to the N-side is through F, cutting (F,G) effectively cuts all paths from N to K that would then go to G.
    *   Paths to G via X: Similar to K, these paths must come from F (e.g., N-...-F-X-G). Cutting (F,G) also cuts these paths.

4.  **Formulate a cut set based on these entry points:**
    To disconnect N from G, we need to cut all paths that lead to G.
    *   To cut all paths that go through H to G, we must remove the edge (H, G).
    *   To cut all paths that go through F (and consequently K or X) to G, we must remove the edge (F, G).

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    If edges (H, G) and (F, G) are removed:
    *   Any path N - ... - H - G is broken by removing (H, G).
    *   Any path N - ... - F - G is broken by removing (F, G).
    *   Any path N - ... - F - K - G is broken because F is disconnected from G by removing (F, G).
    *   Any path N - ... - F - X - G is broken because F is disconnected from G by removing (F, G).

    *   Removing (H, G) leaves paths like N - O - P - F - G.
    *   Removing (F, G) leaves paths like N - C - H - G.

    *   Removing (X, G) leaves paths like N - O - P - F - G and N - C - H - G and N - O - P - F - K - G.
    Since no single edge removal can disconnect N from G, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2. Our proposed cut set has 2 edges.

    *   Therefore, to completely disconnect D from the rest of the graph (and thus from F), both edges (J, D) and (P, D) must be removed.
5.  **Determine the size of the cut:** Removing (J, D) and (P, D) requires removing 2 edges.
6.  **Check for alternative minimal cuts:**
    *   Consider the nodes J and P, which are the immediate predecessors to D. Any path from F to D must pass through either J or P.
    *   By Menger's theorem, for non-adjacent source and sink nodes (F and D are not adjacent), the minimum edge cut is equal to the minimum vertex cut. If we remove nodes J and P, D becomes isolated. This is a vertex cut of size 2. This implies that the minimum edge cut is also 2.
    *   We need to ensure there isn't another set of 2 edges that disconnects F from D by cutting paths earlier.

        *   Removing (M, J) and (O, P) would still leave paths via I to J or P (e.g., F-E-I-J-D or F-E-I-P-D).
        *   Removing (I, J) and (I, P) would still leave paths via M to J (e.g., F-E-M-J-D) and via O to P (e.g., F-E-B-C-O-P-D).
    *   Thus, cutting the direct connections to D, i.e., (J, D) and (P, D), is the most direct and minimal way to disconnect D from F.

    *   To disconnect H from the rest of the graph, I can remove all edges connected to H: (H, B), (H, G), (H, I). This is also a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., size 1 or 2) is possible:**
    *   A cut of size 1 is not possible, as there are clearly multiple paths between N and H (e.g., N-B-H and N-O-C-I-H).
    *   To check for a cut of size 2, I will attempt to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least 3.

4.  **Find edge-disjoint paths:**
    *   **Path 1:** N - B - H
        *   Edges: {(N, B), (B, H)}

    *   **Path 2:** Now, find a path from N to H that does not use any edges from Path 1.
        *   Start from N, using an unused edge, e.g., (N, O).
        *   N - O - C - I - H
        *   Edges: {(N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, find a path from N to H that does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        *   Edges used so far: {(N, B), (B, H), (N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}.
        *   Start from N, using the remaining unused edge (N, Y).
        *   N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
        *   Let's list the edges for this path and verify disjointness:
            *   Edges: {(N, Y), (Y, O), (O, P), (P, D), (D, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H)}
        *   Check for overlap with Path 1: No common edges.
        *   Check for overlap with Path 2:
            *   Path 2 uses (N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H).
            *   Path 3 uses (N, Y), (Y, O), (O, P), (P, D), ..., (G, H).
            *   The edges connected to O are (N, O) in Path 2 and (Y, O), (O, P) in Path 3. These are distinct edges.
            *   The edges connected to H are (I, H) in Path 2 and (G, H) in Path 3. These are distinct edges.
            *   All other edges in Path 3 are distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.
        *   Therefore, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3. 6.  **Select a minimal cut set:**
    A straightforward minimal cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    Cut Set: [(N, Y), (N, O), (N, B)]
    Removing these three edges isolates node N, ensuring no path exists between N and H.

The final answer is based on the reasoning above.

    *   Node F is connected to K, X, G, and E.

2.  **Consider paths from F to D:**
    To disconnect F from D, we need to ensure that no path exists between them. All paths from F to D must eventually reach D.

3.  **Analyze the connections to node D:**
    Node D has only two edges connected to it: (P, D) and (J, D).
    *   Any path from F to D must use either the edge (P, D) or the edge (J, D) as its final step to reach D.

4.  **Evaluate a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If we remove only one of these edges, for example, (P, D), then paths from F to D via J would still exist (e.g., F-K-J-D).
    *   If we remove only (J, D), then paths from F to D via P would still exist (e.g., F-G-H-I-P-D).
    *   Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect F from D.

5.  **Evaluate a cut set of size 2:**
    *   If we remove both edges (P, D) and (J, D), node D becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   Since D is isolated, no path can exist from F to D.
    *   This cut set, [(P, D), (J, D)], successfully disconnects F from D and consists of 2 edges.

6.  **Confirm minimality:**
    Since 1 edge is not enough, and 2 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges required is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(P, D), (J, D)]

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The task is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect the two black nodes, N and G. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's Theorem is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between N and G.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between N and G:**
    Let's list some representative paths to understand the graph structure:
    *   Path 1: N - B - H - G
    *   Path 2: N - B - E - F - K - G
    *   Path 3: N - B - E - F - X - G
    *   Path 4: N - B - C - I - H - G
    *   Path 5: N - Y - O - P - I - H - G
    *   Path 6: N - Y - O - C - B - H - G
    *   Path 7: N - Y - O - C - B - E - F - K - G
    *   Path 8: N - Y - O - C - B - E - F - X - G
    *   Path 9: N - O - P - I - H - G
    *   Path 10: N - O - C - B - H - G
    *   Path 11: N - O - C - B - E - F - K - G
    *   Path 12: N - O - C - B - E - F - X - G

2.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths:**
    We aim to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths.

    *   **Path A:** N - B - H - G
        Edges: `{(N,B), (B,H), (H,G)}`

    *   **Path B:** Now, let's find a path that does not use any of the edges from Path A.
        Consider: N - Y - O - P - I - C - B - E - F - K - G
        Edges: `{(N,Y), (Y,O), (O,P), (P,I), (I,C), (C,B), (B,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G)}`
        Let's verify if Path A and Path B are edge-disjoint:
        -   (N,B) vs (N,Y) - Different.
        -   (B,H) vs (C,B), (B,E) - Different.
        -   (H,G) vs (K,G) - Different.
        So, Path A and Path B are indeed edge-disjoint. We have found 2 edge-disjoint paths.

    *   **Path C:** Now, let's try to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path A and Path B.
        Edges used by Path A: `{(N,B), (B,H), (H,G)}`
        Edges used by Path B: `{(N,Y), (Y,O), (O,P), (P,I), (I,C), (C,B), (B,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G)}`

        To reach G, a path must use one of the edges (H,G), (K,G), or (X,G).
        Path A uses (H,G). Path B uses (K,G).
        So, for a third path to be edge-disjoint, it must use (X,G).
        To reach (X,G), the path must pass through node F (since X is only connected to F and G).
        To reach F, the path must pass through node E (since F is only connected to E, K, X).
        Therefore, any path to (X,G) must use the edge (E,F).

        Let's try to construct such a path: N - O - C - I - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - X - G
        Edges: `{(N,O), (O,C), (C,I), (I,P), (P,D), (D,J), (J,M), (M,L), (L,E), (E,F), (F,X), (X,G)}`
        Let's check for disjointness with Path B:
        -   Path B uses (E,F). Path C also uses (E,F).
        Since Path B and Path C share the edge (E,F), they are not edge-disjoint.

    This critical observation (that all paths to K-G and X-G must pass through E-F) implies that we cannot find three edge-disjoint paths. If one path uses E-F to reach K-G, and another path uses E-F to reach X-G, they will share the edge E-F. Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 2.

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths. Since we found a maximum of 2 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

4.  **Find a cut set of 2 edges:**
    We need to find two edges whose removal disconnects N and G.
    Based on the analysis that all paths to K-G and X-G must use (E,F), (E,F) is a strong candidate for one of the cut edges.
    If we remove (E,F), all paths to G via K or X are broken.
    The remaining paths to G must go via H. These paths include:
    *   N - B - H - G
    *   N - B - C - I - H - G
    *   N - Y - O - P - I - H - G
    *   N - Y - O - C - B - H - G
    *   N - O - P - I - H - G
    *   N - O - C - B - H - G
    All these remaining paths use the edge (H,G) to reach G.
    Therefore, if we remove (E,F) and (H,G), all paths from N to G will be broken.

5.  **Verify the cut set [(E,F), (H,G)]:**
    *   Removing (E,F) breaks all paths that go through E-F to reach K or X. This covers all paths ending in (K,G) or (X,G).
    *   Removing (H,G) breaks all paths that go through H to reach G. This covers all paths ending in (H,G).

    This cut set has 2 edges, which is the minimum possible.

*   Path A (N - B - H - G) is cut by (H,G).
*   Path B (N - Y - O - P - I - C - B - E - F - K - G) is cut by (E,F).

    *   Node B is connected to: O, N, C, E.

2.  **Explore initial cut possibilities:**

    *   To disconnect B, we could remove all edges directly connected to it: (B, O), (B, N), (B, C), (B, E). This would be a cut set of 4 edges.
    The cut set of 3 edges is currently the best candidate.

3.  **Check for a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 edge?** No, because M has 3 distinct neighbors and B has 4. There are clearly multiple independent paths. For example, M-O-B is one path, and M-J-I-C-B is another. Removing one edge cannot break both.
    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 edges?** Let's try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B. If we can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

4.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    Let's look for paths that do not share any common edges.
    *   **Path 1:** M - O - B
        Edges: {(M, O), (O, B)}
    *   **Path 2:** M - J - I - C - B
        Edges: {(M, J), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
    *   **Path 3:** M - L - E - B
        Edges: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, B)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The edges in Path 1 are (M, O) and (O, B).
    *   The edges in Path 2 are (M, J), (J, I), (I, C), and (C, B).
    *   The edges in Path 3 are (M, L), (L, E), and (E, B).

    Comparing the sets of edges, there are no common edges between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these are indeed 3 edge-disjoint paths.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

6.  **Confirm the cut set:**
    We already identified a cut set of 3 edges: [(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)].
    If these three edges are removed, node M becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from M to B.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have found a cut set of 3 edges, and we have proven that at least 3 edges are required, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   This path is a direct connection through a single intermediate node.

    *   Nodes in this path (excluding G and I): {F, M, L, E, B, C}

        *   None of {F, M, L, E, B, C} are H.
        *   None of {F, M, L, E, B, C} are K or J.
    *   So, Path 3 is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal successfully disconnects G and I. I will choose one node from each of the identified vertex-disjoint paths.

*   From Path 1: **H**
*   From Path 2: **J** (I could also choose K, but J is fine)
*   From Path 3: **B** (I could also choose F, M, L, E, or C, but B is a good candidate as it's a bottleneck for other paths as well)

2.  **Removing J**: This breaks all paths that go through J to reach I.

I will try to find a set of node-disjoint paths between E and I. If I find `k` such paths, then the minimum cut size is at least `k`. Then I will try to find a cut set of size `k`.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 5:** Now I need to find a set of 5 white nodes whose removal disconnects E and I. I will select one node from each of the node-disjoint paths identified above, ensuring they are distinct.

    *   Node E (black) is connected to: F, L, M, I, B.
    *   Node J (black) is connected to: M, D, P, I.

2.  **Analyze the structure and potential "bottlenecks":**
    All paths from E to J must eventually pass through one of J's direct neighbors (M, D, P, I).
    *   Node D is only connected to J and P. If paths through P are cut, D becomes a dead end for any path from E to J.
    *   Therefore, the critical "entry points" to J from the rest of the graph are M, I, and P. Any path from E to J must pass through one of these three nodes.

3.  **Consider a cut set by removing edges directly connected to J:**
    To disconnect J from the rest of the graph (and thus from E), we can remove all edges connecting J to its critical neighbors M, I, and P.
    *   Edge (M, J)
    *   Edge (I, J)
    *   Edge (P, J)

4.  **Verify this cut set:**
    Let's check if removing these three edges disconnects E from J.
    *   **Paths through M:** Any path E - ... - M - J would be broken by removing (M, J). Examples: E-M-J, E-L-M-J.
    *   **Paths through I:** Any path E - ... - I - J would be broken by removing (I, J). Examples: E-I-J, E-B-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-F-G-H-B-I-J.
    *   **Paths through P:** Any path E - ... - P - J would be broken by removing (P, J). Examples: E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-N-O-P-J, E-F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J.
    After removing (M, J), (I, J), and (P, J), node J is only connected to node D. Node D is connected to J and P. Since (P, J) is removed, D is effectively only connected to J. There are no paths from E to D. Therefore, J is completely disconnected from E.
    This cut set, [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)], has a size of 3.

5.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible (e.g., 2 edges):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If I can find 3 paths that share no common edges, then by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed.
    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J. Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - I - J. Edges: {(E, I), (I, J)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - O - P - J. Edges: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)}

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   (E, M) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (M, J) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (E, I) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (I, J) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (E, B) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (B, C) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (C, O) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (O, P) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (P, J) is unique to Path 3.
    All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them is at least 3. We have found a cut set of size 3. Thus, 3 is the minimum.

    *   Paths through I: N-C-I-P, N-C-B-I-P, N-C-H-I-P, N-B-I-P.
    *   Paths through E: N-B-E-P, N-C-B-E-P, N-O-E-P, N-Y-O-E-P, N-B-E-M-J-P, N-C-B-E-M-J-P, N-O-E-M-J-P, N-Y-O-E-M-J-P.
    *   Paths through F: N-C-H-G-F-P, N-C-H-G-K-F-P, N-C-H-G-X-F-P.
    *   Paths through J: N-O-E-M-J-P, N-Y-O-E-M-J-P, N-B-E-M-J-P, N-C-B-E-M-J-P.

5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1, 2, or 3:**
    *   **Size 1:** Removing any single node (e.g., O, I, E, F, J, C, B, H, G, M, Y, L, K, X, D) will leave multiple paths open. For example, removing O leaves paths like N-C-I-P, N-B-E-P, N-C-H-G-F-P. Thus, a single node is not enough.
    *   **Size 2:** Removing any two nodes (e.g., {O, I}) will still leave paths open. For example, {O, I} leaves paths like N-B-E-P, N-C-H-G-F-P. Thus, two nodes are not enough.
    *   **Size 3:** Let's systematically test combinations of 3 nodes, focusing on the nodes directly connected to P (O, I, E, J, F) as these are critical "entry points" to P.
        *   **Cut Set: {O, I, E}**
            *   Paths through O, I, E are broken.
            *   Remaining direct connections to P are J and F.
            *   Can N reach J? J is connected to P, M, D. M is connected to E (removed), L, J. So, N cannot reach M, and thus cannot reach J.
            *   Can N reach F? Path N-C-H-G-F-P is still active (C, H, G, F are not removed).
            *   Therefore, {O, I, E} is not a cut set.
        *   **Cut Set: {O, I, F}**
            *   Paths through O, I, F are broken.
            *   Remaining direct connections to P are E and J.
            *   Can N reach E? Path N-B-E-P is still active (B, E are not removed). Path N-C-B-E-P is also active.
            *   Therefore, {O, I, F} is not a cut set.
        *   **Cut Set: {O, E, F}**
            *   Paths through O, E, F are broken.
            *   Remaining direct connections to P are I and J.
            *   Can N reach I? Path N-C-I-P is still active (C, I are not removed). Path N-B-I-P is also active.
            *   Therefore, {O, E, F} is not a cut set.
        *   **Cut Set: {I, E, F}**
            *   Paths through I, E, F are broken.
            *   Remaining direct connections to P are O and J.
            *   Can N reach O? Path N-O-P is still active (O is not removed). Path N-Y-O-P is also active.
            *   Therefore, {I, E, F} is not a cut set.
    *   Based on these systematic checks, it appears that no set of 3 nodes is sufficient to disconnect N and P.

6.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 4:**
    *   Let's consider the set of nodes {O, I, E, F}. These are four of the five nodes directly connected to P.
    *   **Cut Set: {O, I, E, F}**
        *   If O is removed, all paths N-...-O-P are broken.
        *   If I is removed, all paths N-...-I-P are broken.
        *   If E is removed, all paths N-...-E-P are broken.
        *   If F is removed, all paths N-...-F-P are broken.
        *   After removing {O, I, E, F}, the only remaining direct connection to P is J.
        *   Can N reach J?
            *   J is connected to P, M, D.
            *   M is connected to E (removed), L, J. Since E is removed, M can only be reached via L, but L is not connected to N's side of the graph.
            *   Let's trace from N:
                *   N-Y: Y is connected to O (removed). No path to J.
                *   N-C: C is connected to B, H, O (removed), N.
                    *   C-B: B is connected to N, C, E (removed), I (removed). No path to J.
                    *   C-H: H is connected to C, I (removed), G. G is connected to F (removed), K, X. No path to J.
                *   N-B: B is connected to N, C, E (removed), I (removed). No path to J.
        *   Since N cannot reach J (or M, which is the only way to reach J from N's side after removing E), N is completely disconnected from P.

7.  **Conclusion:** A cut set of size 4, {O, I, E, F}, successfully disconnects N and P. Since we have systematically shown that no cut set of size 3 works, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 4.

        *   In both cases (G-K... or G-X...), any path to I must eventually pass through F. Once at F, these paths then follow the same routes as the "Paths starting with G-F" described above (i.e., through F-P or F-E).

    *   **(G, H)**: This edge is essential for all paths that use H as the first step from G.
    *   **(F, P)**: This edge is essential for all paths that use F (directly or via K/X) and then P to reach I.
    *   **(F, E)**: This edge is essential for all paths that use F (directly or via K/X) and then E to reach I.

    Therefore, removing these three edges **{(G, H), (F, P), (F, E)}** will disconnect G from I.

4.  **Verify minimality:**

    *   If **(F, P)** and **(F, E)** are removed: A path like G-H-I still exists (using (G, H)).

    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I
    *   Neighbors of N: B, C, Y, O

3.  **Analyze the structure for potential cut nodes:**
    *   Notice that node B is a common neighbor to both H and N (H-B-N). This is a direct path.
    *   Node Y is a neighbor of N, but it is only connected to N and O. There are no paths from H to Y without passing through B, C, or O first. Therefore, Y is not a critical bridge node for disconnecting H from N. All paths from H to N must eventually pass through B, C, or O.

4.  **Formulate a hypothesis for a cut set:**
    Since all paths from H to N must eventually pass through one of N's effective "entry points" (B, C, O), removing these three nodes should disconnect H from N. Let's test this hypothesis.
    *   If we remove B: The direct path H-B-N is broken.
    *   If we remove C: Any path going H -> ... -> C -> N is broken. For example, H -> I -> C -> N.
    *   If we remove O: Any path going H -> ... -> O -> N is broken. For example, H -> I -> P -> O -> N or H -> G -> K -> J -> P -> O -> N.

5.  **Determine minimality using Menger's Theorem:**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find vertex-disjoint paths between H and N (paths that share no intermediate nodes).

    *   **Path 1:** H -> B -> N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B}

    *   **Path 2:** H -> I -> C -> N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, C}

    *   **Path 3:** H -> G -> K -> J -> P -> O -> N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {G, K, J, P, O}

    Let's check if these sets of intermediate nodes are disjoint:
    *   {B}
    *   {I, C}
    *   {G, K, J, P, O}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are completely disjoint. This means we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found a cut set of size 3 ({B, C, O}) and we have proven that at least 3 nodes are required to disconnect H and N (by finding 3 vertex-disjoint paths), the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   Node G is connected to: X, K, F, H.
    *   Node I is connected to: E, H, B, C, J, P.

2.  **Analyze paths from G to I:**
    Any path starting from G must first traverse one of its outgoing edges: (G, X), (G, K), (G, F), or (G, H).
    *   Nodes X and K are only connected to G and F. They do not offer independent paths to I. If a path goes G-X or G-K, it must then go to F to proceed towards I (e.g., G-X-F-E-I).
    *   Therefore, all paths from G to I must pass through either node F or node H.

3.  **Consider potential cut edges based on this observation:**
    *   If a path goes G -> F -> ..., it uses the edge (G, F).
    *   If a path goes G -> H -> ..., it uses the edge (G, H).

4.  **Test for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If we remove (G, F): Paths like G-H-I still exist.
    *   If we remove (G, H): Paths like G-F-E-I still exist.
    *   If we remove any other single edge (e.g., (H, I), (E, I), (F, E)), there will always be alternative paths between G and I. For example, removing (H, I) leaves G-F-E-I, G-H-B-I, etc.
    *   Thus, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect G from I. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

5.  **Test for a cut of size 2:**
    Based on the observation that all paths from G to I must pass through either F or H, removing the edges that connect G to F and G to H should disconnect G from I.
    *   Let's consider removing the edges **(G, F)** and **(G, H)**.
        *   After removing (G, F), G is no longer connected to F.
        *   After removing (G, H), G is no longer connected to H.
        *   Since all paths from G to I must originate by going through either F or H, and G is now disconnected from both F and H, G is completely disconnected from I.
        *   G remains connected to X and K, but neither X nor K are connected to I, nor can they reach I without passing through F or H (which G is now disconnected from).

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Removing the two edges (G, F) and (G, H) successfully disconnects G from I. Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, a cut of size 2 is the minimum.

The final answer is structured as follows:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, F), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

    *   Neighbors of H: E, G, B, I. (G leads to K, F, X, which are not connected to N).
    *   Neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y. (Y is only connected to N and O, but O is connected to P, which is connected to J, M, E, H. Y is not connected to H via any other path than N itself).

2.  **Focus on the "entry/exit" points for paths between H and N.**
    All paths from H to N must pass through one of H's relevant neighbors (E, B, I) and one of N's relevant neighbors (B, C, O).

3.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths between H and N.**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (the edge connectivity), I can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and N, according to Menger's theorem.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        *   Edges: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        *   Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   Edges: (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses edges (H, B) and (B, N).
    *   Path 2 uses edges (H, I), (I, C), and (C, N).
    *   Path 3 uses edges (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), and (O, N).

    None of the edges in Path 1 are shared with Path 2 or Path 3.
    None of the edges in Path 2 are shared with Path 1 or Path 3.
    None of the edges in Path 3 are shared with Path 1 or Path 2.
    Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size.**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

5.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges.**
    A straightforward way to disconnect N from the rest of the graph (and thus from H) is to remove all edges directly connected to N, except for (N, Y) since Y is not connected to H through any other path.
    The edges connecting N to the "H-side" of the graph are:
    *   (B, N)
    *   (C, N)
    *   (O, N)

    Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects H and N:
    *   Path H - B - N is broken by removing (B, N).
    *   Path H - I - C - N is broken by removing (C, N).
    *   Path H - I - P - O - N is broken by removing (O, N).
    *   Path H - E - M - J - P - O - N is broken by removing (O, N).
    *   Path H - E - M - J - P - C - N is broken by removing (C, N).
    *   Any other path from H to N would eventually have to pass through B, C, or O to reach N, and thus would be cut by one of these edges.

    Alternatively, we could remove the edges directly connected to H that lead towards N:
    *   (H, B)
    *   (H, I)
    *   (H, E)

2.  **Strategy: Find Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** According to Menger's Theorem (vertex version), the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (a minimum vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. I will try to find as many vertex-disjoint paths as possible.

3.  **Trace Vertex-Disjoint Paths from G to N:**
    *   **Path 1:** Start from G.
        G is connected to H, F, K, X.

        Let's try starting from G -> F.
        G -> F -> E -> B -> N.
        Intermediate nodes used: {F, E, B}.
        This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (no common intermediate nodes).

    *   **Path 3:** Find a path from G to N that does not use any of {H, I, C, F, E, B}.

        G -> K -> J -> D -> P -> O -> N.

    *   F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> C -> N -> O -> P -> J

3.  **Paths through M to J:**
    *   F -> G -> H -> C -> B -> E -> M -> J
    *   F -> G -> H -> C -> B -> E -> L -> M -> J

    *   F -> P -> I -> H -> C -> B -> E -> M -> J (This path uses P and M)
    *   F -> P -> I -> H -> C -> B -> E -> L -> M -> J (This path uses P and M)

As established, any path through D must first pass through P.
Therefore, all paths from F to J must pass through either P or M.

*   If I remove only P, paths through M (e.g., F -> G -> H -> C -> B -> E -> M -> J) would still exist.
*   If I remove only M, paths through P (e.g., F -> P -> J) would still exist.

*   All paths that go through P are broken.
*   All paths that go through M are broken.
*   Since all paths from F to J must pass through either P or M (or D, which implies P), removing both P and M will disconnect F and J.

    *   Node L is connected to M and E.
    *   Node H is connected to G, B, and I.

2.  **Consider the simplest approach: isolating one of the target nodes.**
    To completely disconnect node L from the rest of the graph, all edges connected to L must be removed.
    *   The edges connected to L are (L, M) and (L, E).
    *   If we remove both (L, M) and (L, E), node L becomes isolated. This means no path can start from L and reach H.
    *   This cut set has a size of 2: `[(L, M), (L, E)]`.

3.  **Verify if a smaller cut (size 1) is possible:**
    *   If only one edge connected to L is removed (e.g., (L, M)), L is still connected to E via (L, E). From E, there are paths to H (e.g., L-E-B-H). Thus, L and H would still be connected.
    *   Similarly, if only (L, E) is removed, L is still connected to M via (L, M). From M, there are paths to H (e.g., L-M-F-G-H). Thus, L and H would still be connected.
    *   Therefore, removing only one edge is not sufficient to disconnect L from H.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum number of edges:**
    Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and I have found a cut of size 2, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

5.  **Alternative cut set of size 2 (for completeness, though not required for the answer):**
    Let's consider another set of edges that would disconnect L and H.
    All paths from L must pass through either M or E. All paths to H must pass through G, B, or I.

    Consider removing edges (M, F) and (E, B):

    *   Removing (E, B) breaks all paths that go through E-B (L-M-E-B-...-H and L-E-B-...-H).

    *   L is connected to M and E.
    *   J is connected to K, I, P, and D.

    *   **Path 2**: L - E - B - C - O - P - J. The intermediate nodes are {E, B, C, O, P}.
    These two sets of intermediate nodes ({M, K} and {E, B, C, O, P}) are completely disjoint. This means that any single node removal cannot break both of these paths. Therefore, according to Menger's theorem, at least 2 nodes must be removed to disconnect L and J.
4.  **Find a Minimum Cut Set**: Since the minimum is at least 2, I will try to find a cut set of size 2.

        *   If node M is removed, L can only connect to E.
        *   If node E is also removed, L has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph.
        *   Therefore, L is completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, including J.
    *   This set of nodes, [M, E], successfully disconnects L from J.
5.  **Conclusion**: Since we established that at least 2 nodes are required, and we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

This is equivalent to finding the minimum cut between H and N, which by Menger's Theorem, is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and N.

I will systematically identify edge-disjoint paths from H to N. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edges.

1.  **Path 1: H - B - N**

    From C, I can go to N (edge `(C, N)` is available).
    Edges used: `{(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}`
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: H - G - F - E - M - J - I - P - O - N**
    I look for a third path from H to N that does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
    Edges used by P1 and P2: `{(H, B), (B, N), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}`.
    Starting from H, I must use `(H, G)` (as `(H, B)` and `(H, I)` are used by P1 and P2 respectively).
    From G, I can go to F (edge `(G, F)` is available).
    From F, I can go to E (edge `(F, E)` is available).
    From E, I can go to M (edge `(E, M)` is available).
    From M, I can go to J (edge `(M, J)` is available).
    From J, I can go to I (edge `(J, I)` is available).
    From I, I cannot go to C (as `(I, C)` is used by Path 2). I can go to P (edge `(I, P)` is available).
    From P, I can go to O (edge `(P, O)` is available).
    From O, I can go to N (edge `(O, N)` is available).
    Edges used: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, P), (P, O), (O, N)}`
    Let's verify that this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
    - Path 1: `{(H, B), (B, N)}` - No common edges with Path 3.
    - Path 2: `{(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}` - No common edges with Path 3.
    Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection.
A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges directly connected to it. Node N has three direct connections: (B, N), (C, N), and (O, N).
If I remove these three edges:

3.  The path H - G - F - E - M - J - I - P - O - N is broken by removing (O, N).
By removing these three edges, node N becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, ensuring no path exists between H and N. This cut set has a size of 3.

Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of 3 edges, this is the minimum number of edges required.

First, let's identify all possible paths from M to B:
1.  M - E - B
2.  M - L - E - B

        This set successfully breaks all paths from L to J.

        Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, P)]
        This set also successfully breaks all paths from L to J.

    *   **Option C: Cut one edge from each "main branch" of paths.**

        Cut Set: [(L, M), (E, P)]
        This set also successfully breaks all paths from L to J.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have found multiple cut sets of size 2, and we've established that a cut of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. I will provide one of the valid cut sets.

The final answer is based on removing the two edges directly connected to L, which ensures L is completely disconnected from the rest of the graph.

The final answer is:

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and G (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from G.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node N is connected to: O, Y, B, C.
    *   Node G is connected to: H, X, K, F.

2.  **Analyze the overall structure and potential "bottlenecks":**
    The graph can be conceptually divided into three main regions:
    *   **N-side:** Nodes that are "closer" to N and not directly connected to G's immediate neighbors. This includes N, O, Y, C, M, J, D, P, L.
    *   **G-side:** Nodes that are "closer" to G and not directly connected to N's immediate neighbors. This includes G, H, X, K, F.
    *   **Intermediate nodes:** Nodes that bridge the N-side and G-side. These are I, B, E.

3.  **Identify edges connecting the "Intermediate nodes" to the "G-side":**
    Any path from N (in the N-side) to G (in the G-side) must pass through the intermediate nodes {I, B, E} and then cross into the G-side. Let's list all edges that connect a node in {I, B, E} to a node in {H, X, K, F}:
    *   (I, H) - connects I to H
    *   (B, H) - connects B to H
    *   (B, F) - connects B to F
    *   (E, F) - connects E to F

    These four edges are the *only* connections from the set of intermediate nodes {I, B, E} to the set of G's neighbors {H, X, K, F}.

4.  **Formulate a cut set based on these edges:**
    If we remove all four of these edges: `(I, H), (B, H), (B, F), (E, F)`, then no path can cross from the intermediate nodes {I, B, E} to the G-side nodes {H, X, K, F}. Since all paths from N must eventually pass through {I, B, E} to reach {H, X, K, F} and then G, removing these four edges will completely disconnect N from G.
    This cut set has a size of 4.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm that 4 is the minimum, I need to show that removing any *three* of these four edges would leave at least one path between N and G. Let's test this:
    *   If we remove `(I, H), (B, H), (B, F)`, the edge `(E, F)` remains. A path like N-O-M-L-E-F-G would still exist.
    *   If we remove `(I, H), (B, H), (E, F)`, the edge `(B, F)` remains. A path like N-B-F-G would still exist.
    *   If we remove `(I, H), (B, F), (E, F)`, the edge `(B, H)` remains. A path like N-B-H-G would still exist.
    *   If we remove `(B, H), (B, F), (E, F)`, the edge `(I, H)` remains. A path like N-C-I-H-G would still exist.

    Since removing any three of these four edges leaves a path open, all four edges are necessary to break all paths at this specific "bottleneck". Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

6.  **Consider alternative cut sets (e.g., directly from N):**
    Another potential cut set would be to remove all edges directly connected to N: `(N, O), (N, Y), (N, B), (N, C)`. This also results in a cut set of size 4, as N would be isolated. This confirms that a cut of size 4 is achievable.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(I, H), (B, H), (B, F), (E, F)]
