2.  **Explore Paths and Identify Bottlenecks:** I will systematically trace paths from E to J.
    *   From E, the direct neighbors are M, L, F, B.
    *   From J, the direct neighbors are M, D, P, I. (Node D is a dead-end for paths from E, as it only connects to J and P, but P is also connected to J, so D is not a critical path node for E-J disconnection).

3.  **Attempt Single Node Cut:**
    *   If I remove M: Paths E-M-J and E-L-M-J are broken. However, paths through F (e.g., E-F-K-G-H-I-J) and paths through B (e.g., E-B-N-Y-O-P-J) remain. So, M alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove I: Paths E-F-K-G-H-I-J and E-B-C-I-J and E-B-H-I-J are broken. However, paths E-M-J and E-B-N-Y-O-P-J (and similar paths through P) remain. So, I alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove P: Paths E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J are broken. However, paths E-M-J and E-F-K-G-H-I-J (and similar paths through I) remain. So, P alone is not enough.
    *   No single node can disconnect E and J. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

4.  **Attempt Two Node Cut:**
    *   Consider removing two nodes, for example, M and I.
        *   Removing M breaks: E-M-J, E-L-M-J.
        *   Removing I breaks: E-F-X-K-G-H-I-J, E-F-K-G-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J.
        *   However, paths like E-B-N-Y-O-P-J still exist. So, [M, I] is not enough.
    *   Consider removing M and P.
        *   Removing M breaks: E-M-J, E-L-M-J.
        *   Removing P breaks: E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J.
        *   However, paths like E-F-K-G-H-I-J still exist. So, [M, P] is not enough.
    *   Consider removing I and P.
        *   Removing I breaks: E-F-X-K-G-H-I-J, E-F-K-G-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J.
        *   Removing P breaks: E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J.
        *   However, paths like E-M-J still exist. So, [I, P] is not enough.
    *   It appears a two-node cut is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm the minimum cut size, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between E and J (excluding E and J themselves).
    *   **Path 1 (P1): E - M - J** (Intermediate node: M)
    *   **Path 2 (P2): E - F - K - G - H - I - J** (Intermediate nodes: F, K, G, H, I)
        *   P2 is vertex-disjoint from P1 (no common intermediate nodes).
    *   **Path 3 (P3): E - B - N - Y - O - P - J** (Intermediate nodes: B, N, Y, O, P)
        *   P3 is vertex-disjoint from P1 (no M) and P2 (no F, K, G, H, I).

    Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between E and J, by Menger's theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect them.

6.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Based on the three vertex-disjoint paths, I can choose one intermediate node from each path to form a cut set.
    *   From P1: M
    *   From P2: I (or F, K, G, H)
    *   From P3: P (or B, N, Y, O)

    Let's choose the set **[M, I, P]**.
    *   Removing M breaks P1 (E-M-J and E-L-M-J).
    *   Removing I breaks P2 (E-F-X-K-G-H-I-J and E-F-K-G-H-I-J). It also breaks other paths that use I, such as E-B-C-I-J and E-B-H-I-J.
    *   Removing P breaks P3 (E-B-N-Y-O-P-J, E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J).

    Let's verify that all paths are broken by [M, I, P]:
    *   Any path from E to J must pass through one of J's neighbors (M, P, I, D). Since D is not reachable from E without passing through P, all paths must pass through M, P, or I.
    *   If a path uses M, it's broken by M.
    *   If a path uses P, it's broken by P.
    *   If a path uses I, it's broken by I.
    Therefore, the set [M, I, P] successfully disconnects E and J.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I also found a cut set of size 3 ([M, I, P]) that successfully disconnects E and J. Thus, 3 is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H. This is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H, according to Menger's Theorem.

First, I will identify all direct and indirect paths between E and H. Then, I will attempt to find a set of edge-disjoint paths. The number of such paths will be the minimum number of edges to remove. Finally, I will propose a cut set of that size and verify its effectiveness.

Let's list potential edge-disjoint paths from E to H:

1.  **Path 1: E - H**
    This is a direct connection.
    Edges: `(E, H)`

2.  **Path 2: E - F - H**
    This path goes through the common neighbor F.
    Edges: `(E, F), (F, H)`
    These edges are distinct from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: E - G - H**
    This path goes through the common neighbor G.
    Edges: `(E, G), (G, H)`
    These edges are distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.

At this point, we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths. E is still connected to L and M. H is still connected to B and I. We need to find more paths that do not use any of the edges already identified: `(E, H), (E, F), (F, H), (E, G), (G, H)`.

4.  **Path 4: E - L - M - J - I - H**
    This path uses the following edges: `(E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)`.
    Let's check if any of these edges are already used:
    *   `(E, L)`: Not used.
    *   `(L, M)`: Not used.
    *   `(M, J)`: Not used.
    *   `(J, I)`: Not used.
    *   `(I, H)`: Not used.
    All edges in Path 4 are distinct from Paths 1, 2, and 3.

We have successfully identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is at least 4.

Now, I need to verify if there is a 5th edge-disjoint path.
Edges used so far:
*   `(E, H)` (from Path 1)
*   `(E, F), (F, H)` (from Path 2)
*   `(E, G), (G, H)` (from Path 3)
*   `(E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)` (from Path 4)

Remaining outgoing edges from E: `(E, M)`
Remaining incoming edges to H: `(H, B)` (since (H,F), (H,G), (H,I), (H,E) are used or part of used paths)

Let's try to construct a 5th path starting with `(E, M)` and ending with `(B, H)`.
From M, the neighbors are L and J.
*   `(M, L)` is used in Path 4.
*   `(M, J)` is used in Path 4.
Since both outgoing edges from M (excluding (M,E)) are already used in Path 4, there is no way to extend a path from E-M without reusing an edge. This means we cannot find a 5th edge-disjoint path.

Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 4. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Now, I will identify a cut set of 4 edges that disconnects E from H.
Based on the paths identified, a good strategy is to cut the "first" edge of each path, or a critical bottleneck.

Consider the following cut set:
1.  `(E, H)`: Cuts Path 1 directly.
2.  `(E, F)`: Cuts Path 2 (and any other path starting E-F...).
3.  `(E, G)`: Cuts Path 3 (and any other path starting E-G...).
4.  `(M, J)`: This edge is a bottleneck for all paths from E through L or M to the right side of the graph (J, I, P, D, C, O, N, B, Y) which eventually lead to H.

Let's verify that removing these 4 edges `[(E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (M, J)]` completely disconnects E from H:
*   After removing `(E, H)`, the direct path is broken.
*   After removing `(E, F)`, E can no longer reach F, thus breaking all paths through F (e.g., E-F-H, E-F-K-G-H).
*   After removing `(E, G)`, E can no longer reach G, thus breaking all paths through G (e.g., E-G-H, E-G-K-F-H).
*   After removing `(M, J)`, E is still connected to L and M (via `(E, L)` and `(E, M)`). However, M can no longer reach J. Since J is the only node connecting M (and by extension L) to the rest of the right-hand side of the graph (I, P, D, C, O, N, B, Y), removing `(M, J)` effectively isolates the {E, L, M} component from the rest of the graph.
    *   E is connected to L and M.
    *   L is connected to E and M.
    *   M is connected to E and L.
    *   Since `(M, J)` is removed, M has no other connections to the graph.
    *   Therefore, the component {E, L, M} is completely disconnected from H.

All paths from E to H are broken by this set of 4 edges. Since we established that 4 is the minimum number of edges required, this cut set is minimal.

    *   Node E is connected to B, F, and L. If we remove the three edges (E, B), (E, F), and (E, L), node E becomes isolated, and thus disconnected from H. This is also a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a Smaller Cut (e.g., size 2) is Possible:** To prove that 3 is the *minimum* number of edges, we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

4.  **Search for Edge-Disjoint Paths from H to E:**
    *   **Path 1:** H - X - F - E
        *   Edges: {(H, X), (X, F), (F, E)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - K - F - B - E
        *   Edges: {(H, G), (G, K), (K, F), (F, B), (B, E)}
        *   Let's verify if Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint:
            *   Initial edges from H: (H, X) vs (H, G) - distinct.
            *   Intermediate edges: (X, F) vs (G, K), (K, F), (F, B) - distinct.
            *   Final edges to E: (F, E) vs (B, E) - distinct.
            *   So far, Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - J - M - L - E
        *   Edges: {(H, I), (I, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E)}
        *   Let's verify if Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
            *   Initial edges from H: (H, I) is distinct from (H, X) and (H, G).
            *   Final edges to E: (L, E) is distinct from (F, E) and (B, E).
            *   Intermediate edges: (I, J), (J, M), (M, L) are distinct from all intermediate edges in Path 1 ((X, F)) and Path 2 ((G, K), (K, F), (F, B)).
            *   Therefore, Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion on Minimum Cut Size:** Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. As we already identified cut sets of size 3 (e.g., removing all edges connected to H, or all edges connected to E), the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 3.

6.  **Select a Cut Set:** A simple and valid cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes. I will choose the edges connected to E.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are M and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect M and B, without removing M or B themselves.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of M:** L, E, J.
2.  **Identify all direct neighbors of B:** E, G, H, C, N.

Now, let's trace all possible paths from M to B and identify critical nodes.

**Paths originating from M can be categorized by their first step:**

*   **Paths starting with M -> L:**
    *   M -> L -> E -> B
    *   M -> L -> E -> G -> B
    *   M -> L -> E -> G -> H -> B
    *   M -> L -> E -> F -> G -> B
    *   M -> L -> E -> F -> K -> G -> B
    *   M -> L -> E -> F -> X -> K -> G -> B
    *   *Observation:* All paths starting with M -> L must pass through node E.

*   **Paths starting with M -> E (direct):**
    *   M -> E -> B
    *   M -> E -> G -> B
    *   M -> E -> G -> H -> B
    *   M -> E -> F -> G -> B
    *   M -> E -> F -> K -> G -> B
    *   M -> E -> F -> X -> K -> G -> B
    *   *Observation:* These paths directly involve node E.

*   **Paths starting with M -> J:**
    *   M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B
    *   M -> J -> P -> O -> N -> B
    *   M -> J -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B
    *   M -> J -> P -> I -> C -> B
    *   M -> J -> P -> I -> H -> B
    *   M -> J -> P -> I -> H -> G -> B
    *   *Observation:* All paths starting with M -> J must pass through node P (since D is a dead end for reaching B).

**Analyzing the "bottlenecks":**

*   From the observations above, any path from M to B must pass through either node E (if it starts M -> L or M -> E) OR node J (if it starts M -> J).
*   Alternatively, any path from M to B must pass through either node E (if it starts M -> L or M -> E) OR node P (if it starts M -> J, as J's only path to B is through P).

**Finding the minimum cut set:**

1.  **Can we disconnect with 1 node?**
    *   If we remove E, all paths through L and E are broken. However, paths through J (e.g., M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B) remain.
    *   If we remove J, all paths through J are broken. However, paths through L and E (e.g., M -> E -> B) remain.
    *   If we remove P, all paths through J are broken. However, paths through L and E (e.g., M -> E -> B) remain.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes must be at least 2.

2.  **Can we disconnect with 2 nodes?**
    *   Consider removing nodes E and J.
        *   Removing E breaks all paths that go M -> L -> E -> ... -> B and M -> E -> ... -> B.
        *   Removing J breaks all paths that go M -> J -> ... -> B.
        *   Since all paths from M to B must pass through either E or J, removing both E and J will disconnect M from B.
        *   This forms a cut set of size 2: [E, J].

    *   Consider removing nodes E and P.
        *   Removing E breaks all paths that go M -> L -> E -> ... -> B and M -> E -> ... -> B.
        *   Removing P breaks all paths that go M -> J -> P -> ... -> B (as P is the only node J connects to that leads to B).
        *   Since all paths from M to B must pass through either E or P, removing both E and P will disconnect M from B.
        *   This also forms a cut set of size 2: [E, P].

Since we have found a cut set of size 2, and we've established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

2.  **Consider a potential cut set:**
    If we remove all edges directly connected to H, namely (G, H), (I, H), and (B, H), then H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from E to H. This cut set has 3 edges.

3.  **Determine if this is the minimum number of edges:**
    To confirm that 3 is the minimum, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
        Edges used: (E, G), (G, H)
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H

    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        Edges used: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

    *   Path 1: (E, G), (G, H)
    *   Path 2: (E, B), (B, H)
    *   Path 3: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)
    All edges listed are unique and do not overlap between the three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them must be at least 3. As we have already identified a cut set of 3 edges ([(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]) that successfully disconnects E and H, this is indeed the minimum.

Path 1: L - M - J
This path uses node M.

Path 2: L - E - B - H - I - J
This path uses nodes E, B, H, I. It is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (except L and J).

1.  L - M - J

*   Any other path starting L-M-... is also broken.
*   L is now only connected to E.
*   J is now connected to D, P, I.

*   E - B - N - Y - O - P

1.  Removing M breaks all paths that go through M, including L-M-J and any paths like L-M-F-...-J.
2.  After removing M, L is only connected to E. E is connected to B, which is now removed. So, L becomes isolated from the rest of the graph.
3.  Since L is isolated, no path can exist from L to J.

This confirms that removing M and B disconnects L from J. Since we established that at least two nodes are required, [M, B] is a minimum cut set.

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of F and J:**
    *   F is connected to P, G, K, X.
    *   J is connected to P, I, M, D.

        *   Edges: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
        *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses none of the edges (F, P) or (P, J).

    Since we have found two edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

3.  **Attempt to find a third edge-disjoint path:**
    Let's consider the edges used by Path 1 and Path 2: {(F, P), (P, J), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)}.

            *   F - K - G ... (but (G, H) is used in Path 2, and (G,H) is the only way from G to H, which leads to I and then J).

            *   F - X - G ... (but (G, H) is used in Path 2).

        *   To reach M from F, we would need to go through P (e.g., F - P - E - M - J). But (F, P) is used in Path 1.
        *   To reach D from F, we would need to go through P (e.g., F - P - D - J). But (F, P) is used in Path 1.

    It appears that all paths from F to J must use either (F, P) or eventually pass through G, then H, then I. Since (G,H) and (H,I) are used in Path 2, and (I,J) is used in Path 2, and (P,J) is used in Path 1, it is not possible to construct a third edge-disjoint path.

    Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 2, which means the minimum cut is 2.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 2:**
    Based on the analysis, we need to find two edges whose removal disconnects F and J.
    Consider the edges:
    *   **(F, P)**: This edge is the direct link from F to node P, which is a major hub for paths to J.
    *   **(G, H)**: This edge is a critical link in the chain F-(G,K,X)-G-H-I-J.

    *   **Remove (F, P)**:

        *   F can still reach G via (F, G).
        *   F can still reach K via (F, K). K can reach G via (K, G).
        *   F can still reach X via (F, X). X can reach G via (X, G).
        *   So, F can still reach G, K, and X.

        *   X is connected to F, G, K. It cannot reach H without G.
        *   Therefore, the component {F, G, K, X} is completely disconnected from the rest of the graph.
        *   Since J is not in {F, G, K, X}, F is disconnected from J.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

*   All paths from H that start via X or G must pass through node F to reach E.

-   If I remove H, paths through F (e.g., N-O-P-F-G) still exist.
-   If I remove F, paths through H (e.g., N-C-H-G) still exist.
-   If I remove P, paths through H (e.g., N-C-H-G) still exist.

-   If I remove I, paths through C-H (e.g., N-C-H-G) and paths through P-F (e.g., N-O-P-F-G) still exist.

According to Menger's theorem, if there are 'k' vertex-disjoint paths between two nodes, then at least 'k' nodes must be removed to disconnect them. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

3.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 3**: Based on the lower bound, I will now attempt to find a cut set of size 3. I will consider nodes that act as "gateways" or "bottlenecks" close to the target node E. The direct neighbors of E are B, F, L, M.

    *   **Paths through B**: Any path from I to E that passes through node B (e.g., I-C-B-E, I-O-N-B-E, I-J-D-P-O-N-B-E, I-C-O-Y-N-B-E) will be broken by the removal of B.

5.  **Conclusion**: Since all paths from I to E are broken by removing nodes B, F, and M, and we previously established that at least 3 nodes are required, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

2.  **Explore all possible paths between E and I:**
    I will systematically identify distinct paths from E to I. To determine the minimum cut, it's helpful to find vertex-disjoint paths, as per Menger's theorem.

    Let's find three vertex-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1 (P1):** E -> M -> J -> P -> I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}
    *   **Path 2 (P2):** E -> N -> Y -> O -> C -> I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {N, Y, O, C}
    *   **Path 3 (P3):** E -> F -> K -> G -> H -> I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G, H}

3.  **Verify vertex-disjoint property:**
    *   P1 uses {M, J, P}.
    *   P2 uses {N, Y, O, C}.
    *   P3 uses {F, K, G, H}.
    None of these sets of intermediate nodes overlap. Therefore, these three paths are indeed vertex-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of vertices whose removal disconnects two non-adjacent vertices (vertex-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are F and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from J.

2.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them. If we can find N edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least N. If we then find a cut set of size N, that is the minimum.

3.  **Identify Edge-Disjoint Paths from F to J:** I will systematically search for paths from F to J, ensuring that each new path does not share any edges with previously identified paths.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): F - K - M - J**
        *   Edges in P1: (F,K), (K,M), (M,J)

    *   **Path 2 (P2): F - G - H - I - J**
        *   Edges in P2: (F,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,J)
        *   *Check for disjointness with P1:* P2 uses nodes G, H, I, which are distinct from K, M used in P1. The edges (F,G) and (F,K) are distinct. The edges (I,J) and (M,J) are distinct. Thus, P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3 (P3): F - E - B - C - O - P - J**
        *   Edges in P3: (F,E), (E,B), (B,C), (C,O), (O,P), (P,J)
        *   *Check for disjointness with P1 and P2:* P3 uses nodes E, B, C, O, P, which are distinct from K, M (P1) and G, H, I (P2). The edge (F,E) is distinct from (F,K) and (F,G). The edge (P,J) is distinct from (M,J) and (I,J). All intermediate edges are also distinct. Thus, P1, P2, and P3 are all edge-disjoint.

4.  **Conclusion from Edge-Disjoint Paths:** We have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between F and J. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect F and J.

5.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect F and J. A straightforward approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes.
    *   Node J has three direct connections: (J,P), (J,I), and (J,M).
    *   If these three edges are removed, node J becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from F to J.

6.  **Final Determination:** We have found a cut set of 3 edges ([(J,P), (J,I), (J,M)]), and we have proven that the minimum number of edges required is at least 3. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect G from N. This is a minimum cut problem, and by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will try to identify edge-disjoint paths from G to N.
1.  **Path 1:** G - E - B - N
    Edges in this path: `(G, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, N)`

2.  **Path 2:** G - H - I - C - N
    Edges in this path: `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, N)`

3.  **Path 3:** G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - Y - N
    Edges in this path: `(G, F)`, `(F, E)`, `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, Y)`, `(Y, N)`

Now, I will check if these three paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges).
*   The first edges from G are `(G, E)`, `(G, H)`, and `(G, F)`. These are distinct.
*   The next edges are `(E, B)` (from Path 1), `(H, I)` (from Path 2), and `(F, E)` (from Path 3). These are distinct.
*   The next edges are `(B, N)` (from Path 1), `(I, C)` (from Path 2), and `(E, L)` (from Path 3). These are distinct.
*   Path 2 continues with `(C, N)`. Path 3 continues with `(L, M)`. These are distinct from each other and from Path 1's remaining edges.
*   Path 3 continues with `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, O)`, `(O, Y)`, `(Y, N)`. All these edges are unique to Path 3 and do not appear in Path 1 or Path 2.

Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between G and N, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and N is 3.

Next, I need to identify a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it. Node N has three direct connections: `(N, B)`, `(N, C)`, and `(N, Y)`.

Let's verify this cut set:
If the edges `(N, B)`, `(N, C)`, and `(N, Y)` are removed, node N becomes isolated. No path can exist from G (or any other node) to N, as N has no remaining connections. This set contains 3 edges.

Since we have established that the minimum cut size is 3, and we have found a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects G and N, this is the correct answer.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]

    *   **Paths starting E -> B:** These paths directly enter the subgraph containing B, H, I, N, C, O, P, and eventually lead to J. Examples: E-B-H-I-J, E-B-N-O-P-J.
    *   **Paths starting E -> M:** M is connected to E, L, and F. Any path from E through M that aims to reach J must eventually pass through F. For example, E-M-F-...
    *   **Paths starting E -> L:** L is connected to E and M. Any path from E through L that aims to reach J must eventually pass through M, and then F. For example, E-L-M-F-...

3.  **Identify critical "bottlenecks" from E:** From the analysis above, it's clear that all paths originating from E and leading towards J must pass through either node F or node B. The nodes L and M only serve as intermediate steps that eventually lead to F (or back to E).
    *   If a path starts E-L, it must go E-L-M. From M, it can only go to F (M-F) to continue towards J, or back to E (M-E or M-L-E).

    *   Therefore, E, L, and M form an isolated component, unable to reach any part of the graph that leads to J.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (E,F), then E can still reach J via B (e.g., E-B-H-I-J).
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (E,B), then E can still reach J via F (e.g., E-F-K-J).
    *   Removing any other single edge connected to E (like (E,L) or (E,M)) would still leave paths through F and B open.

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I. This is a minimum cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes.

First, I will identify a set of edge-disjoint paths between G and I. If I can find N edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least N.

Let's identify paths from G to I:

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**
    Edges: (G, H), (H, I)

2.  **Path 2: G - F - K - J - I**
    Edges: (G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I)
    This path uses different edges from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: G - E - B - C - I**
    Edges: (G, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I)
    This path uses different edges from Path 1 and Path 2.

4.  **Path 4: G - B - O - P - I**
    Edges: (G, B), (B, O), (O, P), (P, I)
    Let's check for edge-disjointness with the previous paths:
    *   (G, B) is distinct from (G, H), (G, F), (G, E).
    *   (B, O) is distinct from (H, I), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I).
    *   (O, P) is distinct from all previous edges.
    *   (P, I) is distinct from all previous edges.
    This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3.

I have identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between G and I. Therefore, according to Menger's theorem, at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect G and I.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that actually disconnects G and I. A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
Node I is connected to H, J, C, and P. The edges are (H, I), (J, I), (C, I), and (P, I).
If I remove these four edges:
*   (H, I) breaks Path 1 (G-H-I).
*   (J, I) breaks Path 2 (G-F-K-J-I) and any other path ending in J-I (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-I, G-E-F-X-K-J-I).

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify direct connections and common neighbors:**
    *   Node E is directly connected to L, M, P, B.
    *   Node J is directly connected to M, D, P, I.
    *   Nodes M and P are common neighbors of E and J, indicating direct paths: E-M-J and E-P-J.

2.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J:**
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - C - I - J
    *   **Path 4:** E - B - H - I - J
    *   **Path 5:** E - B - C - O - P - J (Note: This path uses P, which is also used in Path 2, but the path segments leading to P are different)
    *   **Path 6:** E - B - H - G - F - P - J (Note: This path also uses P)

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths.

    *   **Path A:** E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path B:** E - P - J (Edges: (E, P), (P, J))
    *   **Path C:** E - B - C - I - J (Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J))

    Let's check if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in Path A: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   Edges in Path B: {(E, P), (P, J)}
    *   Edges in Path C: {(E, B), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J)}

    All edges listed are distinct. For example, (E, M) is not (E, P) or (E, B). (M, J) is not (P, J) or (I, J). And so on. Therefore, these three paths (A, B, C) are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and J is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    To disconnect J from E, we can remove all edges directly connecting J to any node that can reach E. These nodes are M, P, and I.
    Consider the cut set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)].
    *   Removing (J, M) breaks Path 1 (E-M-J).
    *   Removing (J, P) breaks Path 2 (E-P-J), Path 5 (E-B-C-O-P-J), and Path 6 (E-B-H-G-F-P-J).
    *   Removing (J, I) breaks Path 3 (E-B-C-I-J) and Path 4 (E-B-H-I-J).

    After removing these three edges, J is only connected to D. Node D is a leaf node connected only to J, and it has no path to E. Therefore, J is completely disconnected from E.

    This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the minimum determined by Menger's Theorem.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]

    Let's consider the immediate neighbors of N: {B, C, O, Y}.

        *   G - K - F - E - ... (then follows paths from E)

        This cluster {G, X, K, F} is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, including N.

        *   If we remove [C, X]: Paths through C are broken. Paths through X/G are broken. However, paths through I-J (e.g., H-I-J-M-O-N) still exist. So [C, X] is not enough.
    *   It appears that a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient. This suggests the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Finding Vertex-Disjoint Paths):** To confirm that the minimum cut is at least 3, I will try to find 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N (excluding H and N themselves).
    *   **Path A:** H - C - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {C}
    *   **Path B:** H - I - J - P - Y - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {I, J, P, Y}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path A.
    *   **Path C:** H - X - K - F - B - N
        *   Intermediate nodes: {X, K, F, B}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path A and Path B.

    Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect H and N is at least 3.

6.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Based on the identified paths, I need to select three nodes that will break all of them.
    *   To break Path A (H-C-N), I must remove **C**.
    *   To break Path B (H-I-J-P-Y-N) and all other paths starting H-I-J, I can remove **J** (or I, P, or Y).
    *   To break Path C (H-X-K-F-B-N) and all other paths starting H-X-K-F or H-G-X-K-F, I can remove **F** (or X, K, or B).

    Let's propose the cut set: **[C, J, F]**.

7.  **Verify the Proposed Cut Set [C, J, F]:**
    *   **Removing C:** Breaks Path 1 (H-C-N) and Path 7 (H-I-C-N).
    *   **Removing J:** Breaks all paths that pass through J. This includes Path 2, 3, 4, 5, 6. After removing J, there are no remaining paths from H through I.
    *   **Removing F:** Breaks all paths that pass through F. This includes Path 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13. After removing F, there are no remaining paths from H through X or G.

    Since all initial branches from H (through C, I, X, and G) are now severed from N, H is completely disconnected from N.

8.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3, and [C, J, F] is one such cut set.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, J, F]

    Let's list some representative paths to understand the connections:
    *   Path 1: G - H - B - N
    *   Path 2: G - H - I - C - N
    *   Path 3: G - H - I - P - O - N
    *   Path 4: G - E - H - B - N
    *   Path 5: G - E - H - I - C - N
    *   Path 6: G - E - H - I - P - O - N
    *   Path 7: G - E - M - J - P - O - N

3.  **Consider removing edges directly connected to N:**
    To completely disconnect N from the rest of the graph (and thus from G), we can remove all edges connecting N to other nodes that are part of paths from G. These edges are (N, B), (N, C), and (N, O).

    *   **Hypothesis: Cut Set S1 = [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]**
        *   Removing (N, B) breaks all paths ending in B-N (e.g., Path 1, Path 4).
        *   Removing (N, C) breaks all paths ending in C-N (e.g., Path 2, Path 5).
        *   Removing (N, O) breaks all paths ending in O-N (e.g., Path 3, Path 6, Path 7).
        This set of 3 edges successfully disconnects N from G.

4.  **Check for a smaller cut set (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**
    *   **Can 1 edge disconnect G and N?** No, because there are multiple independent routes to N (via B, C, and O). Removing one edge like (N, B) would still leave paths through C and O.
    *   **Can 2 edges disconnect G and N?** Let's try removing any two edges from S1:
        *   If we remove (N, B) and (N, C), a path through O still exists (e.g., G - E - M - J - P - O - N).
        *   If we remove (N, B) and (N, O), a path through C still exists (e.g., G - H - I - C - N).
        *   If we remove (N, C) and (N, O), a path through B still exists (e.g., G - H - B - N).
        Since removing any two edges directly connected to N is not sufficient, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 2 edges are not sufficient, and we found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects G from N, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

*   **Two Node Cut?**

    Therefore, removing both **E** and **I** will sever all connections between J and F.

    1.  Removing `E` breaks all paths in Group 1.

1.  **Paths through node E**: These paths eventually reach node E and then connect to F via the edge (E,F).
2.  **Paths through nodes H, X, G, K**: These paths eventually reach one of these nodes and then connect to F. All these nodes (H, X, G, K) are interconnected and also connected to F.

**Group 1: Paths through E**
All paths from J that go through E must use the edge (E,F) to reach F. Examples include:
*   J-M-L-E-F
*   J-M-O-N-B-E-F
*   J-I-C-B-E-F
Removing the edge (E,F) will effectively cut all paths in this group.

**Group 2: Paths through H, X, G, K**
These paths do not use E. Instead, they must reach F through one of the nodes H, X, G, or K. Let's examine how J connects to these nodes:
*   J is connected to I.
*   I is connected to H and C.
*   C is connected to H.
Therefore, any path from J to H (and subsequently to X, G, K, and F) must pass through either the edge (I,H) or the edge (C,H).
*   Path J-...-I-H-...-F: This path uses the edge (I,H).
*   Path J-...-I-C-H-...-F: This path uses the edge (C,H).

If we remove both (I,H) and (C,H), then node H becomes unreachable from J. Since H is the only node that connects to X, G, and K from the "J-side" (i.e., from I or C), if H is unreachable, then X, G, and K also become unreachable from J. Consequently, F becomes unreachable from J via this group of paths.

**Proposed Cut Set:**
Based on this analysis, a minimal set of edges to remove would be:
1.  **(E,F)**: To cut all paths in Group 1.
2.  **(I,H)**: To cut paths from J to H via I.
3.  **(C,H)**: To cut paths from J to H via I-C.

Let's verify this cut set: [(E,F), (I,H), (C,H)].
*   If (E,F) is removed, no path can reach F through E.
*   If (I,H) and (C,H) are removed, no path from J can reach H. Since H is the gateway to X, G, and K from the J-side, no path from J can reach X, G, or K either. Therefore, no path can reach F through H, X, G, or K.

Since all paths from J to F are covered by either Group 1 or Group 2, and our proposed cut set breaks all paths in both groups, J and F will be completely disconnected.

The number of edges in this cut set is 3.
To confirm this is the minimum, I consider that a vertex cut of {E, H} would disconnect J from F (removing E cuts all paths through E, removing H cuts all paths through H, X, G, K). A minimum vertex cut of size 2 implies a minimum edge cut of at least 2. A more precise vertex cut would be {E, H, X}. If we remove these three nodes, J and F are disconnected. This implies that the minimum edge cut is at least 3. Since we found an edge cut of size 3, it must be the minimum.

    As established in step 3, removing only one edge connected to D is insufficient because D has two distinct connections. Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

6.  **Consider alternative cut sets of size 2:**
    Could there be two edges further away from D that would also disconnect E and D?
    All paths to D must pass through J or P.
    *   Paths to J originate from M and I.
    *   Paths to P originate from I and O.
    To disconnect D, we would need to cut all paths leading to J AND all paths leading to P.
    *   To cut all paths to J, we would need to remove at least (M, J) and (I, J). (2 edges)
    *   To cut all paths to P, we would need to remove at least (I, P) and (O, P). (2 edges)
    If we try to cut upstream, for example, by removing (M, J) and (I, P):
        *   Path E-M-J-D is broken.
        *   Paths E-...-I-P-D are broken.
        *   However, paths like E-...-I-J-D still exist (e.g., E-F-G-H-I-J-D).
        *   Also, paths like E-...-O-P-D still exist (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-D).
    This demonstrates that finding two edges further upstream that simultaneously cut all paths to J and all paths to P is difficult and likely requires more than 2 edges due to the graph's connectivity. The most direct and efficient way is to cut the edges directly connected to D.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    The minimum number of edges required is 2, and the cut set is [(J, D), (P, D)].

The final answer is structured as requested.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between G and I:**
    First, I list the direct neighbors of G and I:
    *   Neighbors of G: K, X, F, E, H
    *   Neighbors of I: H, E, J, P, C

    Now, I trace paths from G to I. I observe that all paths from G to I must pass through either node E or node H, as these are the only nodes connected to G that also have connections leading to I (E is connected to I, H is connected to I). Let's verify this:
    *   If a path starts G -> E, it can then go E -> I (Path 1) or E -> H -> I (Path 2).
    *   If a path starts G -> H, it can then go H -> I (Path 3) or H -> E -> I (Path 4).
    *   If a path starts G -> F, F is connected to E. So, G -> F -> E -> I (Path 5) or G -> F -> E -> H -> I (Path 6).
    *   If a path starts G -> K, K is connected to F. So, G -> K -> F -> E -> I (Path 7) or G -> K -> F -> E -> H -> I (Path 8).
    *   If a path starts G -> X, X is connected to F. So, G -> X -> F -> E -> I (Path 9) or G -> X -> F -> E -> H -> I (Path 10).

    In all these cases, to reach I, the path must eventually use either the edge (E, I) or the edge (H, I).

2.  **Identify critical edges for disconnection:**
    Based on the observation above, the edges (E, I) and (H, I) are critical.
    *   The edge (E, I) is the direct connection from node E to node I.
    *   The edge (H, I) is the direct connection from node H to node I.

3.  **Formulate a cut set:**
    To completely disconnect G from I, I must ensure that no path can reach I from G. Since all paths from G to I must pass through either E or H (and then directly to I), removing both (E, I) and (H, I) will sever all these paths.
    *   Removing (E, I) breaks all paths that use E to reach I (e.g., G-E-I, G-F-E-I, G-K-F-E-I, G-X-F-E-I, and also G-E-H-I because E can no longer reach I).
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that use H to reach I (e.g., G-H-I, and also G-E-H-I because H can no longer reach I).

    Therefore, the set of edges [(E, I), (H, I)] will disconnect G from I.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    Consider two distinct paths between G and I:
    *   Path A: G - E - I
    *   Path B: G - H - I
    These two paths are edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges). According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since there are at least two edge-disjoint paths, at least two edges must be removed.
    Our proposed cut set [(E, I), (H, I)] contains exactly two edges. Since it successfully disconnects G from I and we know at least two edges are required, this is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, I), (H, I)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify all possible paths from G to N:**
    To systematically explore, I'll consider the immediate neighbors of G: F, H, K, X. All paths from G must start by traversing one of the edges (G,F), (G,H), (G,K), or (G,X). Similarly, all paths to N must end by traversing one of the edges (B,N), (C,N), (O,N), or (Y,N).

    Let's categorize paths based on the first step from G:

    *   **Paths starting G-H:**
        *   G-H-B-N
        *   G-H-I-C-N
        *   G-H-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N
        *   G-H-I-J-P-O-Y-N
        *   G-H-I-J-D-P-O-Y-N

    *   **Paths starting G-F (or G-K-X-F, G-X-F, as K and X connect to F):**
        *   G-F-E-B-N
        *   G-F-M-L-E-B-N
        *   G-K-X-F-E-B-N
        *   G-X-F-E-B-N

2.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" edges:**
    To disconnect G from N, we need to cut all these paths. I'll look for a minimal set of edges that, when removed, ensures no path remains.

    Consider the connections from the "G-side" of the graph to the "N-side".
    *   All paths originating from G and passing through H must eventually use either edge (H,B) or edge (H,I) to proceed towards N. If both (H,B) and (H,I) are removed, all paths starting G-H are cut.
    *   All paths originating from G and passing through F (or K/X which lead to F) must eventually use edges (F,E) or (F,M) to proceed.
        *   If a path goes G-...-F-E-..., it will eventually reach N via B (i.e., E-B-N).
        *   If a path goes G-...-F-M-..., it will eventually reach N via L and E (i.e., M-L-E-B-N).
        Notice that all paths from the F/K/X branch that lead to N eventually converge on node E, and then from E to B, and then to N. Therefore, cutting the edge (E,B) would sever all paths from the F/K/X branch to N.

3.  **Propose a cut set and verify:**
    Based on the analysis above, let's propose the following cut set of 3 edges:
    *   **(H,B)**: This cuts all paths from G-H that go directly to B.
    *   **(H,I)**: This cuts all paths from G-H that go through I (and then to C, J, P, O, Y, and eventually N).
    *   **(E,B)**: This cuts all paths from G-F/K/X that go through E (and then to B, and eventually N).

    Let's verify that removing these three edges disconnects G from N:
    *   If (H,B) is removed, the path G-H-B-N is broken.
    *   If (H,I) is removed, all paths starting G-H-I... (e.g., G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N, G-H-I-J-P-O-Y-N, G-H-I-J-D-P-O-Y-N) are broken because H can no longer reach I.
    *   If (E,B) is removed, all paths starting G-F/K/X... that pass through E (e.g., G-F-E-B-N, G-F-M-L-E-B-N, G-K-X-F-E-B-N, G-X-F-E-B-N) are broken because E can no longer reach B. Since B is the only node E connects to that leads to N, this effectively isolates the F/K/X branch from N.

    With these three edges removed, no path exists from G to N.

4.  **Confirm minimality:**
    To confirm that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that no set of 1 or 2 edges can disconnect G from N.
    *   **Size 1:** Removing any single edge (e.g., (H,B)) would leave many other paths intact (e.g., G-H-I-C-N, G-F-E-B-N). So, 1 edge is not enough.
    *   **Size 2:** Let's try to remove any two edges from our proposed cut set and see if a path remains:
        *   If we remove (H,B) and (H,I), the path G-F-E-B-N still exists.
        *   If we remove (H,B) and (E,B), the path G-H-I-C-N still exists.
        *   If we remove (H,I) and (E,B), the path G-H-B-N still exists.
    Since removing any two edges leaves at least one path, 2 edges are not enough.

    Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are F (black) and D (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F and D.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are F and D.

2.  **Consider a simple cut:** A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges directly connected to it. Node D is connected to J, P, O, and Y.
    *   Edges connected to D: (J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D).
    *   If we remove these 4 edges, node D becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from F. This constitutes a cut set of size 4.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible:** To prove that 4 is the minimum, we can try to find 4 edge-disjoint paths between F and D. According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them.

4.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:** Let's find four paths from F to D that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1:** F → M → J → D
        Edges: (F, M), (M, J), (J, D)

    *   **Path 2:** F → E → B → C → O → D
        Edges: (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, D)

    *   **Path 3:** F → G → H → I → P → D
        Edges: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)

    *   **Path 4:** F → K → I → C → N → Y → D
        Edges: (F, K), (K, I), (I, C), (C, N), (N, Y), (Y, D)

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness:** Let's list all edges from these four paths and check for any overlaps:
    *   From Path 1: (F, M), (M, J), (J, D)
    *   From Path 2: (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, D)
    *   From Path 3: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)
    *   From Path 4: (F, K), (K, I), (I, C), (C, N), (N, Y), (Y, D)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these four paths are distinct. For example:
    *   The edges directly connected to F ((F,M), (F,E), (F,G), (F,K)) are all different.
    *   The edges directly connected to D ((J,D), (O,D), (P,D), (Y,D)) are all different.
    *   All intermediate edges are also unique to their respective paths.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between F and D, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 4. As we previously identified a cut set of size 4 (by removing all edges directly connected to D), this confirms that the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D), (O, D), (Y, D)]

Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 2
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and I (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect all paths between G and I.

First, I identify the direct neighbors of G and I:
*   Neighbors of G: F, X, K, H
*   Neighbors of I: J, P, C, H

I will systematically identify all distinct "channels" or "bottlenecks" through which paths from G can reach I.

1.  **Direct connection via H:**
    *   Path: G - H - I
    *   This path can be cut by removing either (G, H) or (H, I).

2.  **Connection via K and J:**
    *   Paths:
        *   G - K - J - I
        *   G - K - J - P - I
        *   G - X - K - J - I
        *   G - X - K - J - P - I
        *   G - F - X - K - J - I
        *   G - F - X - K - J - P - I
    *   All these paths rely on the connection between K and J. Removing the edge (K, J) would cut all of them.

3.  **Connection via the lower-left network (F, M, L, E, B, N) or (H, B, N) to C/O/P:**
    *   Paths originating from G and going through F:
        *   G - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - I
        *   G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - C - I
        *   G - F - M - L - E - B - N - O - P - I
    *   Paths originating from G and going through H (and then B):
        *   G - H - B - N - C - I
        *   G - H - B - N - O - C - I
        *   G - H - B - N - O - P - I
    *   All these paths converge on node B and then pass through N to reach C, O, or P, which then connect to I. The edge (B, N) is a critical link for all these paths.

Based on this analysis, I propose the following cut set of three edges:
*   **(H, I)**: This cuts the direct path G-H-I.
*   **(K, J)**: This cuts all paths that go through K and J to reach I.
*   **(B, N)**: This cuts all paths that go through the lower-left network (via F-M-L-E-B or H-B) to reach N, and then proceed to I via C, O, or P.

Let's verify if removing these three edges disconnects G from I:
*   If (H, I) is removed, G cannot reach I directly via H. Any path from G to H (e.g., G-H, G-F-M-L-E-B-H) would be blocked from reaching I at H.
*   If (K, J) is removed, G cannot reach J (and thus I) via K (e.g., G-K-J-I, G-X-K-J-I, G-F-X-K-J-I).
*   If (B, N) is removed, any path from G that reaches B (e.g., G-H-B, G-F-M-L-E-B) cannot proceed to N, and thus cannot reach C, O, or P to connect to I.

Therefore, these three edges effectively cut all identified paths from G to I.

Now, I must ensure this is the *minimum* number of edges.
*   If I remove only (H, I) and (K, J), paths like G-H-B-N-C-I would still exist.
*   If I remove only (H, I) and (B, N), paths like G-K-J-I would still exist.
*   If I remove only (K, J) and (B, N), paths like G-H-I would still exist.
Since removing any two edges leaves at least one path, a cut set of size 2 is not possible. Thus, 3 is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (K, J), (B, N)]
